10 Arithmetic Emergence
This section constructs the bounded arithmetic of counts and ratios. (Classical
background for the standard instruments used here: [3]; the derivations below are
self-contained and independent of these sources.)
Definition 10.1 (The Capacity Count) For each stratum Sn of the chain, define its capacity
count
κ(Sn ) := N − n
the number of unallocated partition levels remaining at that stratum. By construction
κ(S0 ) = N (genesis capacity, Axiom 5.3) and κ(S_N) = 0 (exhaustion at the harmonic point,
Definition 5.2). Zero is thereby derived, not posited: it is the closure condition of the descent.
Proposition 10.1 (Well-Definedness and Bijectivity) κ is a bijection between the strata of
the chain and the counts 0, 1, . . . , N.

Proof Totality and surjectivity: the chain has exactly the strata S0 , . . . , S_N (Theorem 5.10,
Corollary 5.11), and κ assigns them N, . . . , 0 respectively. Injectivity: distinct strata have
distinct depths by the strictness of the chain (Corollary 5.11), hence distinct counts.
□
Definition 10.2 (Successor as Allocation) Define the successor s on counts by
s(κ(Sn+1 )) := κ(Sn ), i.e., s(k) = k + 1 for 0 ≤ k < N
Succession is the inverse reading of allocation: the successor of a count is the count of the
coarser stratum from which one partition step descends to it. Succession toward the envelope
is recovery of scope; allocation toward the harmonic point is consumption of capacity.
Theorem 10.2 (The Bounded Successor Structure) The structure ⟨0, . . . , N, 0, s⟩ satisfies:
1. Zero is a count: 0 = κ(S_N), derived as exhaustion (Definition 5.2). 2. Zero is not
a successor: s(k) = k + 1 ≥ 1 for all k in the domain of s; structurally, the harmonic point
has no finer stratum (Theorem 5.10). 3. The successor is injective: from the injectivity of the
chain (Corollary 5.11, Proposition 10.1). 4. Bounded induction: any property that holds at

18

0 and is preserved by s holds for every count in 0, . . . , N. Proof of (4). The chain is finite
and strict (Corollary 5.11). A property holding at the harmonic point and propagated by each
allocation step reaches every stratum in at most N steps; there is no infinite descent and no
stratum outside the chain (Theorem 5.10, Proposition 6.3). □
Remark (Bounded, not unbounded). The structure is the Peano skeleton relativized to
the genesis bound: distinguished zero, injective successor, no cycles, induction — with the
successor terminating at N rather than extending endlessly. This is the no-infinity thesis
(Proposition 6.3) carried into number: a framework in which infinity is structurally impossible
generates 0, . . . , N, not N. Unbounded N is a downstream idealization — the limit vocabulary
of the downstream formulation — not a primitive object. Where Peano posits zero and an
endless successor, Relational Lysis derives zero and a terminating one.
Proposition 10.3 (Derivation of the Magnitude Order) Define k ⪯ k′ ⇐⇒ the stratum of
count k′ enfolds the stratum of count k or coincides with it. Then ⪯ is a total order on 0, . . . ,
N, coinciding with the numerical order, and it is reflexive, antisymmetric, and transitive.

Proof By Proposition 10.1 the correspondence between counts and strata is bijective, and by
Proposition 5.6 and Corollary 5.11 enfolding totally orders the strata: the coarser contains
the finer, and κ is strictly decreasing along containment. Reflexivity is the coincidence clause;
antisymmetry follows from the strictness of containment (Axiom 5.2): mutual enfolding of
distinct strata is impossible; transitivity is the transitivity of enfolding (Axiom 5.2). The
order coincides with numerical order on counts because containment corresponds exactly to
smaller depth, hence larger count. □
Remark (Interface discharge). Proposition 10.3 discharges Axiom RL7 of the downstream
formulation: the magnitude order ⪯, there posited with reflexivity, antisymmetry, and transitivity, is here derived from enfolding. Magnitude is structural depth read numerically —
the intrinsic order of the stratification, not an imported order relation.
□
Definition 10.3 (Bounded Arithmetic) Addition and multiplication are defined on 0, . . . , N
by bounded recursion where the results remain within the bound: k + 0 := k; k + s(j) := s(k
+ j) where defined; k · 0 := 0; k · s(j) := (k · j) + k where defined. Operations exceeding the
genesis bound are undefined: no count beyond capacity exists to serve as a value. Remark.
Partiality is not a defect but the arithmetic expression of Genesis Capacity: the framework’s
number system cannot outrun the levels fixed by the relation. Downstream formulations may
embed 0, . . . , N in N or R as an idealization; the primitive arithmetic is total on its domain
and silent beyond it.
Theorem 10.4 (The Priority of Representation over Number) The envelope representation
is prior to number: every numerical object of Sections 9.1–9.6 is defined from the stratification,
and no numerical object is required to define the stratification.

Proof The chain, its strictness, its finiteness, and its terminal stratum are established in
Section 4 from the axioms of Sections 1–4, none of which quantifies over numbers: capacity
is fixed as a structural property of the bounding relation (Axiom 5.3), not as a numeral.
The counts, zero, successor, order, and operations are then defined from the chain (9.1–9.6).

19

The dependence is strictly one-directional. A single relation represents the entire interior at
the envelope before any count exists; number emerges only as the bookkeeping of its interior
allocation. Representation precedes and does not reduce to counting. □
Remark (Against IT FROM BIT — hypothesis marker). Theorem 10.4 establishes the
priority claim internally: distinction descends from relation; information is not assembled from
binary units upward. The stronger conjecture — that relational representation can finitely
express what integer encoding cannot — remains a hypothesis under investigation, tied to
whether some structural constant of the framework is forced to a value admitting no finite
expansion. It is recorded here as a marked conjecture, not a result.
□
Remark 10.1 (The Rationality Lemma) Every quantity definable from the primitive structure by counting — counts, ratios of counts, and any composition thereof under bounded
arithmetic (Definition 10.3) — is expressible as a ratio of integers.

Proof All counts lie in 0, . . . , N (Proposition 10.1); constituency at every stratum is finite
(Proposition 5.9); bounded arithmetic composes counts by finitely many additions and multiplications where defined. Every such quantity is therefore an integer or a ratio of integers.
□
Consequence. No enumerative quantity of the framework escapes finite integer expression.
If any structural constant is to escape it, the constant must arise relationally — as the unique
coherent solution of a structural condition — and not by counting. The framework’s thesis
that representation precedes counting is thereby load-bearing: only representation, never
enumeration, could ground the conjecture of 9.7’s closing remark.
□

Experimental correspondence: Appendix A.

11 The Conjugate Partition
This section establishes the Conjugate Partition.
Definition 11.1 (Reversal and Parity) The reversal of a mediation is the exchange of its
oriented sides: rev(A ⊗ B) := B ⊗ A. By Axiom 2.4, reversal is nontrivial on every mediation
of distinct terms, and it is involutive: rev(rev(A ⊗ B)) = A ⊗ B. A configuration is aligned
(orientation-even) if it is invariant under reversal, and residual (orientation-odd) if reversal
carries it to its opposite orientation.
Theorem 11.1 (Conjugate Partition of Mediations) Let Comp denote the composite contents of the domain — the mediations, i.e., contents of the form A ⊗ B (Definition 5.1).
Then:
(a) Every mediation is residual. For every m ∈ Comp: rev m ̸= m. (b) Reversal is
a fixed-point-free involution on Comp, and it partitions Comp into two-element
conjugate pairs m, rev m: every mediation belongs to exactly one pair. (c) The two sectors. The residual sector consists of the mediations severally; the aligned sector consists
of the conjugate pairs, each invariant as a pair under reversal. The sectors are exclusive and
jointly exhaustive of the structure: every mediation lies in exactly one conjugate pair, every
conjugate pair is invariant, and no mediation is a pair. —

20

Proof (a) Let m = A ⊗ B ∈ Comp. By Definition 5.1, mediation separates distinct terms:
A ̸= B. By Axiom 2.4, A ⊗ B ̸= B ⊗ A. By Definition 11.1, rev m = B ⊗ A. Therefore rev
m ̸= m. No evaluation of the pair in coincidence is performed at any point: the inequality is
supplied directly by Axiom 2.4, which settles every case uniformly. ■(a)
(b) Involutivity: rev(rev(A ⊗ B)) = rev(B ⊗ A) = A ⊗ B (Definition 11.1, applied twice;
the computation acts at the mediation’s own boundary and is unaffected by any interior
constitution of A or B). Fixed-point-freeness is (a). Define, for each m ∈ Comp, the pair
P(m) := m, rev m. By (a) its two members are distinct; by involutivity both are mediations,
so P(m) ⊆ Comp, and rev m is itself residual since rev(rev m) = m ̸= rev m by (a) applied to
rev m. Disjointness or identity of pairs: suppose P(m) ∩ P(m′ ) ̸= ∅. Then m′ ∈ m, rev m. If
m′ = m, then P(m′ ) = P(m). If m′ = rev m, then rev m′ = rev(rev m) = m, so P(m′ ) = rev
m, m = P(m). In every case P(m′ ) = P(m): distinct pairs are disjoint. Every mediation m
belongs to P(m); therefore the pairs partition Comp, and each mediation belongs to exactly
one pair. ■(b)
(c) Pair-invariance: rev carries P(m) = m, rev m to rev m, m = P(m); each pair is invariant
as a pair while each member varies (by (a)). Exclusivity and exhaustiveness: exhaustiveness
of the residual sector over mediations is (a) — there is no aligned mediation; exhaustiveness
of the pairing is (b) — every mediation lies in exactly one pair; exclusivity between the sectors
is structural — a pair is a two-membered aggregate and a mediation is a single composite,
and no content is both, since the pair’s two members are distinct (a) while a mediation is
one content. ■(c)
Remark (what this theorem does and does not assert). It asserts the primitive decomposition architecture: mediation-level variance, pair-level invariance, exhaustive
conjugate pairing. It does not assert any additive splitting of a single content into alignedplus-residual parts: the domain possesses no addition of contents, and no such operation
is invoked. Where a downstream formulation carries an additive symmetric/antisymmetric
decomposition, that is a rendering available only after value-structures with addition are
introduced there; the present theorem is its primitive ancestor and makes no claim beyond
itself. —
□

Experimental correspondence: Appendix B.

12 Opposition, Reversal, and the Residual Flow
This section establishes opposition, Reversal, and the Residual Flow.
Dependencies. Axioms 2.2, 2.3, 2.4 and 5.3; Definitions 5.1 and 11.1; Propositions 3.1,
5.5, 5.4 and 14.1; Theorem 1; Corollary 5.11 and Section 9.
Theorem 12.1 (Opposition–Reversal Coincidence) On every stratum of the chain, structural
opposition coincides with reversal of mediation:
¬Sn = rev(Sn )

Proof By induction. Base: ¬S0 = ¬(x ⊗ ¬x) = ¬x ⊗ ¬¬x = ¬x ⊗ x (Axiom 2.3) = rev(x ⊗
¬x) = rev(S0 ) (Definition 11.1). Step: ¬Sn+1 = ¬(Sn ⊗ ¬Sn ) = ¬Sn ⊗ Sn (Axiom 2.3) =
rev(Sn ⊗ ¬Sn ) = rev(Sn+1 ). □
Remark. On the generated structure, the opposite of a stratum is its orientation reversal:
the two sides of any stratum are its two orientations. Conjugate distinctness (Proposition 5.3)

21

is thereby re-grounded directly in the nontriviality of reversal (Axiom 2.4), and Interior
Conjugation (Proposition 5.4) acquires its explicit mechanism: the reflected sector is the
reversed orientation.
□
Theorem 12.2 (The Primitive Residual Flow Law) Residual allocation increases by exactly
one unit per partition step: at depth n, the allocated boundary units number n + 1, every unit
is primitively residual, and aligned content arises only derivatively, by conjugate pairing of
previously allocated units.

Proof Each partition step allocates exactly one level of genesis capacity, enacted by exactly
one intercession of the Between (Axiom 5.3, Proposition 5.5); with the envelope’s own boundary this gives n + 1 allocated units at depth n. Every single mediation is orientation-odd
(Theorem 1(a)): each allocated unit enters the residual sector. Aligned content is constituted
only by conjugate pairs (Theorem 1(b)–(c)), and pairs arise only when a previously allocated unit stands together with its reversal — which occurs derivatively, since each stratum
Sn+1 contains Sn together with ¬Sn = rev(Sn ) (Theorem 12.1), presenting every boundary
interior to Sn in both orientations. No step allocates an aligned unit directly. The residual
sector therefore gains one unit per step without exception, while the aligned sector gains only
pairings of what allocation has already provided.
□

Experimental correspondence: Appendix B.

13 Primitive Lysis and the Aligned Flow
This section establishes primitive Lysis and the Aligned Flow.
Dependencies. Definitions 5.1 and 11.1; Proposition 5.4; Theorems 1, 11.1, 11.2;
Corollary 5.11.
Definition 13.1 (Primitive Lysis) Primitive lysis, denoted Λ, is the pairing operation: each
application of Λ pairs the eldest unpaired allocated unit with its reversal, which is interiorly
available (Theorem 12.1, Proposition 5.4), converting one residual single into one aligned
conjugate pair (Theorem 1(b)–(c)). Λ is deterministic (the eldest unpaired unit is unique
by the strict chain, Corollary 5.11), strictly monotone (each application strictly decreases
the count of unpaired units), and finitely terminating (the units are finite, Theorem 12.2).
Remark. Primitive lysis is deterministic, strictly monotone in the unpaired count, and finitely
terminating — the operator-level descent in its primitive form.
Theorem 13.1 (The Primitive Aligned Flow Law) Under lysis accounting at interior depth
n (0 < n < N), the aligned sector holds n conjugate pairs and the residual sector holds exactly
one unpaired unit: at every interior depth, exactly one unit stands unpaired against n paired.

Proof At depth n, the allocated units number n + 1 (Theorem 12.2). Every unit except the
frontier stands together with its reversal in the enclosing stratum (Theorem 12.1 applied at
each enclosing level: each stratum contains its predecessor together with the predecessor’s

22

reversal, presenting every interior unit in both orientations) and is paired by Λ; the frontier
unit’s reversal is not yet presented and it remains unpaired. Hence n pairs and one single. □
—
□

Experimental correspondence: Appendix B.

14 Immutability, Coupling at Genesis, and the
Withdrawal of Verdict 12.5
This section establishes immutability, Coupling at Genesis, and the Withdrawal of
Verdict 12.5.
Proposition 14.1 (Immutability of Solved States) A state, once generated, cannot be
altered. Perturbations do not modify existing states; each perturbation constitutes a new
assertion generating its own solved state, descending from the top of its own stratification.

Proof A state’s identity is entirely constituted by its bounding relation (Definition 5.1): there
is no identity-bearing substrate apart from the relation itself. An "alteration" of a state’s
constitution is therefore not a change within that state but the constitution of a different
state. Interior strata are enfolded (Axiom 5.2) and their generation order is fixed by the
strict chain (Corollary 5.11); no operation of the framework re-enters a completed stratum,
since partition allocates only at the frontier (Proposition 5.5) and lysis only pairs what
allocation has provided (Definition 13.1). A perturbation is an assertion; every assertion forces
representation, relation, and mediation (Definition 3.1, Propositions 3.1 and 4.1), generating
a solved state at the top of a new descent. Alteration cannot occur within the tower; only
decomposition occurs there.
□
Corollary 14.2 (Coupling at Genesis) Every datum of a descent’s constitution is fixed at
its envelope; no interior process can revise it.

Proof Whatever data constitute the envelope’s solved state are part of its constitution, hence
immutable once generated (Proposition 14.1). Interior processes — allocation and pairing —
operate on subsequent strata and cannot re-enter the envelope (Proposition 14.1). The entire
descent inherits the envelope’s constitution as fixed initial data. What couples, couples at
the top.
□

Experimental correspondence: Appendix B.

15 Residence, Instantiation, and the Determination
of N
This section establishes residence, Instantiation, and the Determination of N.

23

Theorem 15.1 (Residence) Every finite configuration resides within the descent of the first
relation: for each finite oriented configuration there is a finite depth at which it stands as
enfolded interior content, independently of any act of selection, retrieval, or observation.

Proof The interior stratification is complete: every level expands into both conjugate sectors
(Proposition 5.6), and orientation gives each partition step its binary valence (Axiom 2.4),
so the stratification is the full oriented tree of finite depth-words. A finite configuration is
a finite oriented word; it is therefore the path-word of a unique stratum at depth equal to
its length, standing as enfolded content of every coarser stratum above it (Proposition 5.8,
Axiom 5.2). Residence requires no observer: the strata exist as the constitution of the descent
(Definition 5.1), not as responses to inquiry. Residence is bounded: each configuration resides
at its own finite depth with finite constituency (Propositions 5.1 and 5.9), and no completed
totality of configurations exists (Proposition 6.3) — pre-existence without infinity. □
Remark. The single line "let x denote the defined" thereby founds the residence of every
finite configuration. What arithmetic cannot do in any single statement — because no numeral
differentiates — the primitive distinction does: it generates the apparatus of differentiation itself, within which all finite content is resident. A work is written before its author
instantiates it: authorship is arrival, not creation.
□
Definition 15.1 (Instantiation) An instantiation is a perturbation-assertion (Proposition 14.1) founding a descent that realizes a resident configuration. Residence is observer-free
existence within the structure; instantiation is the assertion-driven realization of a residence as a solved descent. Residence does not require instantiation; instantiation presupposes
residence.
Definition 15.2 (The Determination Rule — Completion of Axiom 4.3) The genesis
capacity of a descent is the structural depth of the constitution of its asserted configuration:
N = depth of the asserted constitution
Axiom 5.3 asserted that the bounding relation determines capacity at genesis without
stating the rule; the rule is now stated. The assertion founding a descent carries its constitution (Definition 3.1: assertion is the engine of representation); the constitution resides at
a determinate depth (Theorem 15.1); the leveling requirement terminates the descent where
the finest partition matches that constitution’s resolution (Definition 5.2). The determination
is deterministic and observer-free: the specifier is the assertion itself.
Theorem 15.2 (Uniqueness of Capacity) Within any descent, N is unique: assertions of
identical constitution found descents of identical capacity.

Proof By Rule 15.2, capacity is a function of the asserted constitution alone. Identity of
constitution gives identity of residence depth (the path-word of Theorem 15.1 is unique per
configuration), hence of capacity. Immutability (Proposition 14.1) excludes revision after
genesis. Determinism (Axiom 5.3) is thereby restored in full: same constitution, same N,
always.
□

24

Theorem 15.3 (Addressability and Exact Reconstitution) For every resident configuration: its address exists (Theorem 15.1), is unique (the path-word of a stratum is unique by
the strict chain and orientation, Corollary 5.11, Axiom 2.4), and instantiation reconstitutes
the configuration exactly — the realized content coincides with the resident content without
alteration.

Proof Existence and uniqueness as cited. Exactness: a descent founded on the asserted constitution allocates and pairs per Theorems 12.2 and 13.1, terminating at the constitution’s depth
(Rule 15.2); the terminal stratum’s path-word is the configuration itself, and immutability
(Proposition 14.1) certifies that no interior process revises it. Retrieval is arrival at an address;
the copy is the original’s residence, re-realized.
□
Corollary 15.4 (The Universal/Per-Assertion Split) Universal, holding in every descent:
the apparatus of distinction (Sections 1–8) and the arithmetic and logical structure as forms
(Sections 7, 9). Per-assertion: the capacity N and all N-dependent values, determined by
Rule 15.2 from what each descent asserts. Form is universal; depth is assertional.

Proof The universal items are theorems of the axioms alone, holding in every descent by
derivation; N is fixed per descent by Rule 15.2 as the depth of the asserted constitution, and
varies with the assertion by Theorem 15.1’s supply of configurations at every depth.
□
Remark 15.1 (The Plurality of Assertions — interpretive corollary, marked as such) Sections
7 and 9 place logic and arithmetic downstream of distinction; the present section’s reading
extends the same inversion to physics: physical laws and constants are not building blocks
of distinction but the constitution of one assertion, expressed as an instantiated descent.
Residence (Theorem 15.1) then implies that other coherent constitutions — other profiles
of law — reside at their own finite depths, uninstantiated, with equal structural citizenship:
a tower of resident assertions, of which at least one is instantiated. Two constraints give
the reading its content. First, no completed totality of universes exists (Proposition 6.3):
boundedly many instantiated descents, unboundedly addressable alternatives, no completed
all. Second, every resident constitution, whatever its physical profile, carries the universal
sector — the derived logic and the bounded arithmetic. Laws may vary by assertion; the
apparatus of distinction cannot. This is an interpretation of proven structure, not a theorem
asserting other universes; it is recorded with the same status as the IT FROM BIT remark.

Experimental correspondence: Appendix B.

16 The Remaining Axioms: Covariance, Locality,
Composition
This section establishes the Remaining Axioms.
Theorem 16.1 (Covariance — Discharge of RL9) The primitive structure is invariant under
relabeling of supports, exactly and vacuously: labels are not primitive objects.

25

Proof Every primitive item is individuated by constitution alone: a state’s identity is its
bounding relation (Definition 5.1); a stratum is individuated by its oriented path-word
(Theorem 15.1); terms, orientations, and pairings are constitutional data (Definition 11.1,
Theorem 1). No axiom or theorem of the chain mentions a label, name, or coordinate. A labeling is therefore representation-external apparatus; two labelings of one constitution denote
one structure. Invariance under relabeling is not a symmetry the framework possesses but a
dependence it lacks. The downstream covariance axiom is discharged: any labeled realization
inherits exact relabeling invariance from the label-freeness of what it realizes.
□
Theorem 16.2 (Locality — Discharge of RL8) Every primitive operation is confined to a
finite relational neighborhood; indeed to immediate adjacency in the enfolding order.

Proof The operations of the framework are three. Mediation involves exactly two terms —
the Between is binary (Definition 5.1, Proposition 5.9). Allocation acts only at the frontier
stratum (Proposition 5.5, Axiom 5.3): one intercession, within the immediately prevailing
stratum. Lysis pairs the eldest unpaired unit with its reversal, interiorly available in the
immediately enclosing stratum (Definition 13.1, Proposition 5.4). Each operation therefore
touches only containment-adjacent structure: constituents of one stratum and its immediate
container. Interactions confined to finite neighborhoods is thereby discharged in its strongest
form — the primitive interaction radius is one. Finite constituency (Proposition 5.9) bounds
every neighborhood absolutely.
□
Definition 16.1 (Composition — Discharge of RL4) For contents a, b of the domain, define
a ◦ b := a ⊗ b for a ̸= b, a ◦ a := a
Well-definedness and closure. For distinct representables, the joint assertion of a and b is
an assertion; it forces representation, relation, and mediation (Definition 3.1, Propositions 3.1
and 4.1), and the mediation of distinct terms is the composite a ⊗ b — a solved state founding its own descent with the pair as asserted constitution (Proposition 14.1, Rule 15.2). The
composite is bounded, of finite constituency (Proposition 5.9’s induction extends over any
finite mediation), and mediated: representable. For identical arguments, assertion is idempotent — asserting a twice is asserting a — so the diagonal closes trivially. Composition is total
on the domain and closed on representables: RL4 discharged. —

Experimental correspondence: Appendix B.

17 The Securitizer of Distinction, and the Full-Scan
Repairs
This section establishes the Securitizer of Distinction, and the Full-Scan Repairs.
Definition 17.1 (The Binding as Securitizer) The binding of a relational pair into a solved
state (D+S) is the securitizer of distinction: it is what allows distinction to exist without
folding into nullity. Each mediation defeats the collapse = ⇐⇒ ̸= at one point (Proposition 4.1); the binding secures distinction as such — it guarantees that the symmetric and
asymmetric components of every solved state, however D and S differ across the relational

26

manifold, cohere as one pair rather than reopening the nullity. The relational weights of the
binding are what emerge the operator (per the downstream formulation): binding → weights
→ operator. The tower’s descent impacts only the D+S content; the binding is what the
descent presupposes.
Theorem 17.1 (Constancy by Regress) The securitizer is constant: invariant across pairs,
depths, and descents.

Proof Suppose the binding varied with the pair it secures. A varying binding is a distinguished
datum — different here from there — and every distinction requires securing against the nullity (Definition 17.1, Axiom 2.1). The variation would therefore require its own securitizer, and
so on without termination, contradicting the impossibility of infinite regress (Theorem 5.10,
Proposition 6.3). The securitizer must be invariant on pain of regress: constancy is not a
property observed of the binding but constitutive of its office. This derives what Theorem
18.2 exhibited — descent leaves the binding untouched, redistributing content only.
□
Theorem 17.2 (Dimensionlessness by Regress) The securitizer is dimensionless.

Proof Dimension is assertion-content: length, duration, charge are constitutions of particular
assertions, expressed through descents (Rule 15.2). The securitizer is prior to every assertion — it secures the possibility of distinction before anything particular is distinguished.
A dimensioned securitizer would presuppose a distinguished dimensional structure, which it
exists to secure: the same regress as Theorem 17.1.
□
Remark 17.1 (The Securitizer’s Datum — Open) What is proven of the securitizer is its
office, its constancy, and its dimensionlessness; what awaits the proportion axioms is its value.
### 22.B Repairs of Record
Two amendments from the full-scan era remain load-bearing in this chain and are retained;
a third concerned sections since excluded and is noted only as history.
Remark 17.2 (The Coincidence Principle — Relocated to the Representation Tier) The Coincidence principle (Axiom 3.1), which formalizes that an unmediated relation between a term
and its structural opposite is structurally indistinguishable from the assertion of their nondistinction, was in an earlier draft stated here in the repairs section and cited backward by
Proposition 4.1. It is now stated in its proper logical position in Section 3 (Representation),
immediately after the co-standing configuration it concerns and strictly before its first use,
with its non-circularity established there explicitly: the axiom depends only on the founding premise (Definition 2.1) and the collapse it induces (Proposition 2.1), both upstream of
every item that cites it. The development therefore contains no forward dependency edge;
the amendment is conservative and no downstream derivation changes.

Experimental correspondence: Appendix B.

27

18 The Signed Arithmetic of Distinction
This section establishes the Signed Arithmetic of Distinction. (Classical background
for the standard instruments used here: [4]; the derivations below are self-contained
and independent of these sources.)
Definition 18.1 (Summability and the Relational Sum) Contents a, b are summable when
they are distinct and co-asserted: their joint assertion stands (Construction 16.1), so that
mediation between them is admissible (Definition 5.1). For summable a, b, the relational sum
is their mediation:
a ⊞ b := a ⊗ b
— the solved state of the pair, formed through pairwise adjacency exactly as every solved
state is formed. There is no sum of the non-related: for contents that cannot be co-asserted,
no configuration exists in which a sum could stand, and none is defined. On the diagonal, a
⊞ a is not a sum: mediation requires distinct terms (Definition 5.1), and the joint assertion
of a with a is the assertion of a (Construction 16.1, idempotence). Adding a distinction to
itself adds nothing: a sum requires as many distinctions as it counts.
Definition 18.2 (Allocation Units, Orientation, Sign) An allocation unit is one intercession
of the Between allocated by the partition mechanism: exactly one per level (Axiom 5.3,
Proposition 5.5). Every allocation unit is a representational mediation — it mediates a state
with its structural opposite (Proposition 5.5) — and on such mediations opposition coincides
with reversal (Theorem 12.1). Each unit therefore possesses exactly two orientations, itself and
its reversal, distinct by Theorem 1(a). Sign is orientation: the unit in its asserted orientation
is counted +1; its reversal is counted −1. Scope: sign is defined on allocation units; the sum
of Definition 18.1 does not require it.
Theorem 18.1 (Conjugate Genesis: the Signed Count is Born Whole) After n allocations, n
units stand asserted and their n reversed conjugates stand forced: the count structure generated
by assertion is the signed pair (+n, −n) at every n ≥ 1. No unsigned count is primitively
generable; the unsigned naturals are a projection.

Proof By bounded induction (Theorem 10.2) over the chain (Corollary 5.11). Base: the first
allocation asserts one oriented unit (Axiom 5.3, Axiom 2.4); the assertion of the unit forces
the assertion of its structural opposite (Axiom 2.2), which on this representational mediation
is its reversal (Theorem 12.1, Definition 18.2), distinct from it (Theorem 1(a)). Step: each
further allocation adds exactly one asserted unit and forces exactly one further conjugate, by
the same citations; prior units and conjugates persist unaltered (Proposition 14.1). After n
allocations: n asserted units, n forced conjugates.
□
Lemma 18.2 (The Relation-Count) For any content c, let r(c) denote the count of intercessions in its constitution. Then r is well defined; for a bare term r = 0; and each mediation
contributes exactly one intercession.

28

Proof Constituency is finite at every content (Proposition 5.9) and fixed once generated
(Proposition 14.1), so the intercessions of c form a finite, fixed collection, to which the counting of Section 9 applies. A bare term contains no mediation, so r = 0. Each mediation is one
intercession of the Between (Definition 5.1, Axiom 5.3).
□
Theorem 18.3 (The Relational Sum Law — the Copula Counted) Let a, b be summable with
disjoint constitutions: no content is enfolded in both (decidable over their finite constituencies
by Corollary 8.3). Then
r(a ⊞ b) = r(a) + r(b) + 1

Proof The constitution of a ⊗ b comprises the constitutions of a and of b, each enfolded
whole (Axiom 5.2), together with exactly one new intercession — the mediating boundary
itself (Definition 5.1: one mediation, one intercession; Axiom 5.3: one intercession per mediation act). The new boundary is enfolded in neither constituent (Axiom 5.2, asymmetry:
constituents do not contain the composite’s boundary). By disjointness the two enfolded collections share no member, so the intercessions of the composite are the intercessions of a,
the intercessions of b, and the one copula, all distinct; their count is r(a) + r(b) + 1 by the
addition of Section 9 on counts of disjoint finite collections. □
Remark (the copula made visible). The act of summing is itself a relation, and the framework counts it: that is the +1. Classical addition hides the copula; the relational law cannot,
because the copula is the only thing that makes the sum exist. The relation-count r and the
capacity count κ of Section 9 are distinct counters — κ counts remaining partition levels
of one descent; r counts intercessions of a constitution — and no identity between them is
asserted.
□
Lemma 18.4 (Predecessor) Each count k ≥ 1 has a unique predecessor, and for m ≤ n the
count n − m — the result of m predecessor steps from n — is well defined.

Proof On 0, . . . , N the successor is injective with 0 outside its image (Theorem 10.2), so each
k ≥ 1 is the successor of exactly one count — its predecessor. Iterating m ≤ n predecessor
steps from n remains within the chain and is unique at every step; the terminus n − m is
therefore well defined.
□
Theorem 18.5 (Subtraction is Conjugate Pairing) Let a configuration carry n asserted
allocation units, of which m stand conjugate-matched: each of the m together with its reversal presented within the enclosing stratum (Theorem 12.1; the presentation mechanism of
Theorem 13.1). Lysis pairs the matched units deterministically (Definition 13.1), and the
resolved configuration’s unpaired count is n − m (Lemma 18.4). Nothing is removed: the
paired units remain, resolved into aligned conjugate pairs (Theorem 1(b)–(c)); immutability forbids deletion (Proposition 14.1). Subtraction’s domain condition is conjugacy: only
opposite-matched units resolve, exactly as addition’s domain condition is adjacency.

Proof Each application of lysis converts one unpaired matched unit and its reversal into one
aligned pair, decreasing the unpaired count by exactly one predecessor step (Definition 13.1:

29

strictly monotone by one; Lemma 18.4: the step is well defined). After the m available pairings,
no matched unit remains unpaired, and the unpaired count is n − m. The pairs persist as
aligned content (Theorem 1(c)); no content of the configuration has been altered or deleted
(Proposition 14.1) — the difference is a resolution, not a removal. □
Corollary 23.8 (Zero Has No Opposite). [Corrected — see Section 27] Zero is the
exhaustion of capacity (Definition 5.2): the closure condition at which no further admissible
partition exists. Zero is not a distinction: it asserts no term, so Axiom 2.2 forces no conjugate
for it. Every nonzero count is an accumulation of distinctions, each dragging its opposite
into co-assertion (Theorem 18.1); zero uniquely lacks an opposite, because it is closure, not
assertion. The count fact n − n = 0 (Theorem 18.5 with m = n) holds of any configuration
all of whose asserted units stand conjugate-matched and resolve; it is not asserted of the
descent’s own terminal state, whose pairing status is settled in Section 27. □
Correction record. The original corollary identified n − n = 0 with "full pairing at the
harmonic point." That identification was an unexaminedresidue of the withdrawn closurepairing picture (F1) and is refuted by Theorem 22.3: the harmonic point carries exactly one
unpaired unit. The surviving content — zero as capacity exhaustion, zero without opposite
— is unchanged; the erroneous clause is removed.
□
Theorem 18.6 (The Classical Projections) (i) Term projection. Let t(c) count the bare
terms (atoms) in a content’s constitution. For any fully mediated content, r(c) = t(c) − 1,
since every mediation is binary (Definition 5.1, Proposition 5.9): a constitution with t atoms
carries exactly t − 1 intercessions. For summable a, b of disjoint constitutions, t(a ⊞ b) =
t(a) + t(b): the boundary contributes no atom (it is a relation, not a term — Definition 5.1).
Term-counts therefore obey the classical additive law with the copula invisible, and the two
laws are consistent: t(a ⊞ b) − 1 = (t(a) − 1) + (t(b) − 1) + 1. Both projections are
derivable and neither is needed by the framework: the framework’s arithmetic is signed and
copula-counting; the classical laws are what the projections retain. □
Remark (order of emergence). The signed structure precedes the unsigned: integers are
born whole at every assertion (Theorem 18.1), and N is a projection of Z’s positive orientation — the reverse of the classical construction order. Addition is accumulation of oriented
distinctions by mediation; subtraction is their resolution by conjugate pairing; zero is closure.
The two operations are one mechanism read in the two orientations of the Between. —

Experimental correspondence: Appendix B.

19 Multiplication: Counting Through Containment
This section establishes multiplication. (Classical background for the standard instruments used here: [4]; the derivations below are self-contained and independent of these
sources.)
Definition 19.1 (Aggregates) Let a1 , . . . , a_k (k ≥ 2) be pairwise distinct, pairwise
summable contents with pairwise disjoint constitutions (disjointness decidable over finite
constituencies, Corollary 8.3). An aggregate over them is any content obtained by iterated
relational summation (Definition 18.1) exhausting the list — a full mediation tree with the
ai as its leaves. Distinct trees are distinct constitutions: mediation is oriented (Axiom 2.4)

30

and composition is non-associative in constitution (Construction 16.1); the aggregate as a
content depends on the tree.
Lemma 19.1 (Tree Invariance of the Counts) Every aggregate over a1 , . . . , a_k satisfies
t = t(a1 ) + · · · + t(a_k), r = r(a1 ) + · · · + r(a_k) + (k − 1)
independently of the tree.

Proof Induction on the tree. A leaf contributes its own counts. Each internal mediation joins
two disjoint sub-aggregates and, by Theorem 18.3, adds their r-counts plus one copula while,
by Theorem 18.6(i), adding their t-counts exactly (the boundary contributes no atom). A
full binary tree with k leaves has exactly k − 1 internal mediations (the leaf–internal identity
already established in Theorem 18.6’s induction). Summing over the tree gives the stated
counts, which depend only on the leaves and k. □
Remark. The constitution remembers the tree; the counts forget it. Associativity of
addition is therefore a projection fact: true of counts, false of constitutions.
□
Definition 19.2 (Replication and the Relational Product) Let m ≥ 1 and let a1 , . . . , a_k
be pairwise distinct, pairwise disjoint contents each of atom-count t(ai ) = m — instances of a
common size. Such instances exist for any m and k within capacity: Residence (Theorem 15.1)
supplies pairwise distinct configurations of equal depth. A relational product k ⊙ m is any
aggregate over k such instances: a two-level constitution — the outer aggregate over the
instances, the inner constitution within each. The product is defined only where the resulting
counts lie within the capacity bound, inheriting the partiality of Section 9.
Theorem 19.2 (The Product Law) For any relational product k ⊙ m:
t(k ⊙ m) = k·m, r(k ⊙ m) = k·m − 1
with k·m the bounded product of Section 9.

Proof Each instance has t = m; by Lemma 19.1 the aggregate’s atom count is the k-fold sum
m + · · · + m, which is k·m by Section 9’s recursion (multiplication as iterated addition, at
the level of counts). Each instance has r = m − 1 (Theorem 18.6(i)); by Lemma 19.1, r =
k(m − 1) + (k − 1) = k·m − 1 — equivalently, directly by Theorem 18.6(i) applied to the
whole: a fully mediated content of k·m atoms carries k·m − 1 relations. □
Remark (what the product counts). The classical product k·m is the atom count surveyed
through two levels of containment: enfolding is transitive (Axiom 5.2), so the whole counts its
constituents’ constituents. Multiplication is counting through containment — distinction of
distinction. The −1 on the relation side is the same fact as Section 23’s copula made visible
at the second level: however the k·m atoms are gathered, the whole is one solved structure,
and a whole of n atoms carries exactly n − 1 relations.
□
Theorem 19.3 (Commutativity Is a Projection Fact) For k ̸= m: the counts commute —
t and r of k ⊙ m equal those of m ⊙ k (both k·m and k·m − 1, symmetric) — while the
constitutions do not: no relational product k ⊙ m is a relational product m ⊙ k.

31

Proof Count symmetry is Theorem 19.2 with the commutativity of Section 9’s bounded
product. Constitutional distinctness: the immediate constituents of the outer aggregate of k
⊙ m are k contents of atom-count m; those of m ⊙ k are m contents of atom-count k. For
k ̸= m these constituent inventories differ (k copies of size m versus m copies of size k are
equal inventories only when k = m), and contents with different constituent inventories are
different contents, identity being constitution (Definition 5.1).
□
Theorem 19.4 (Distributivity Is a Projection Fact) For summable, disjoint templates of
sizes m and n within capacity: every relational product k ⊙ (m + n) and every aggregate of
(k ⊙ m) with (k ⊙ n) have equal counts — t = k(m + n) = km + kn and r = k(m+n) − 1
(Section 9 distributivity on counts; Lemma 19.1 for the joined aggregate) — while the constitutions differ: k instances of compound size versus a compound of two replications are distinct
trees, hence distinct contents (Axiom 2.4, Definition 5.1). Distributivity, like associativity
and commutativity, holds of the projection and not of the structure.
Theorem 19.5 (What Multiplication Describes, and What It Enforces) Multiplication
describes hierarchical constitution: it is the count of distinctions surveyed through two strata
of enfolding, made possible by the transitivity of containment (Axiom 5.2). It enforces, first,
stratification: the outer contains the inner and never conversely (Axiom 5.2, asymmetry)
— the two factors are not peers but levels; and second, distinctness at both levels: every
replicated instance must be its own secured distinction (Definition 18.1’s diagonal exclusion), and the instances must be pairwise disjoint — no atom counted twice (Theorem 18.3’s
condition). Where addition’s domain condition is adjacency and subtraction’s is conjugacy,
multiplication’s is stratified containment of distinct instances. □
Remark (the tower is not a replication). The tower’s levels are not a product: successive
strata enfold the same prevailing content with its conjugate (Proposition 5.5), violating the
disjointness that Definition 19.2 requires. Replication counts across disjoint instances; descent
refines one instance. The two mechanisms are structurally different, and the arithmetic keeps
them apart: the tower allocates r one unit per level (Theorem 12.2); the product gathers k·m
− 1 relations in one solved whole. —

Experimental correspondence: Appendix B.

20 Division: The Recognition of Stratification
This section establishes division. (Classical background for the standard instruments
used here: [4]; the derivations below are self-contained and independent of these
sources.)
Dependencies. Section 9 (counts, order, bounded arithmetic, bounded induction);
Lemma 18.4 (predecessor); Theorem 18.5 (subtraction as resolution); Section 24 (replication, the product law); Proposition 14.1 (immutability); Theorem 15.1 (Residence);
Definition 5.1. Nothing else is used.
Lemma 20.1 (Monotonicity and Cancellation of Counts) For counts within the bound: (i)
if s ≥ 1 and x + s is defined, then x + s > x; (ii) if d ≥ 1 and x·d = y·d (both defined), then
x = y.

32

Proof (i) x + s is s successor steps from x; each successor step strictly increases in the order
(Theorem 10.2: successor injective, order Proposition 10.3 total on the chain), so x + s > x.
(ii) Suppose x ̸= y; without loss x < y, so y = x + s with s ≥ 1 (Lemma 18.4, iterated). Then
y·d = x·d + s·d (Section 9 recursion) and s·d ≥ d ≥ 1, so y·d > x·d by (i): contradiction. □
Theorem 20.2 (Euclidean Decomposition of Counts) For counts n ≥ 0, k ≥ 1 within the
bound, there exist unique q, rem with
n = k·q + rem, 0 ≤ rem < k

Proof Existence: resolve groups of k by iterated subtraction (Theorem 18.5’s mechanism at
the count level: k predecessor steps per resolution, Lemma 18.4); the count strictly decreases
by k per resolution and the chain is finite, so the iteration terminates at the first value below
k; q is the number of completed resolutions, rem the terminal value. Uniqueness: if k·q + rem
= k·q′ + rem′ with rem, rem′ < k and q < q′ , then q′ = q + s, s ≥ 1, so rem = k·s + rem′
≥ k: contradiction with rem < k (Lemma 20.1(i)); hence q = q′ , and then rem = rem′ . □
Theorem 20.3 (Division Does Not Transform; It Classifies) Division is not an operation
on contents. A content’s constitution is fixed at generation (Proposition 14.1): no act reaggregates an existing whole into instances. What division expresses is recognition: a content
c of atom-count n is k-stratified when its constitution is a relational product over k disjoint
instances (Definition 19.2); it is k-stratifiable in the domain when some constitution of n
atoms in k equal instances resides (Theorem 15.1 supplies one for every factorization n =
k·m within capacity, by Theorem 19.2). For a given content the question "is c divided by k"
is a classification of its actual tree — answered by its constitution, decidably over finite constituency (Corollary 8.3) — not a command to restructure it. The remainder of Theorem 20.2
is the unstratified residue: the distinctions that complete no instance. □
Remark. What division describes about relation: the interrogation of hierarchy — whether
a whole’s relations organize as k-fold containment. Its domain condition is stratification-infact, as multiplication’s is stratification-in-construction. Immutability is why division is the
first operation that can fail on a given content: addition can always extend, but a fixed tree
either is a replication or is not. —

Experimental correspondence: Appendix B.

21 Ratio: The Relation of Accumulations
This section establishes ratio. (Classical background for the standard instruments used
here: [3]; the derivations below are self-contained and independent of these sources.)
Dependencies. Section 9 (counts, bounded arithmetic with its partiality, Addendum 10.1); Lemma 20.1; Theorem 20.2; Definition 3.2 and Construction 16.1
(co-standing and joint assertion); Corollary 8.3. Nothing else is used.
Definition 21.1 (Ratio) A ratio is a comparative relation between two completed counts
held in co-assertion: for counts a and b with b ≥ 1, the ratio a : b is the ordered pair of
accumulations standing in comparison. A ratio is a relation between relations-counts — the

33

framework’s first second-order arithmetic object: it does not count distinctions; it compares
countings. Its domain condition is co-assertion: only counts jointly asserted stand in ratio, as
only adjacent contents stand in sum.
Definition 21.2 (Sameness of Ratio — the Cross Criterion) Ratios a : b and c : d are the
same ratio when a·d = c·b, provided both products are defined within the bound. Comparability is partial: where either cross product exceeds capacity, the comparison is undefined —
the partiality of Section 9 inherited, not a new restriction.
Theorem 21.1 (Sameness Is an Equivalence Where Defined) On ratios whose pairwise cross
products are defined: sameness is reflexive, symmetric, and transitive.

Proof Reflexive: a·b = a·b. Symmetric: immediate from the symmetry of the defining
equation. Transitive: suppose a·d = c·b and c·f = e·d, with the products of the conclusion
defined. Then (a·f)·d = a·(f·d) = a·(d·f) = (a·d)·f = (c·b)·f = (c·f)·b = (e·d)·b = (e·b)·d, using
the associativity and commutativity of the bounded product on counts (Section 9, projection
laws established for counts). Since d ≥ 1, cancellation (Lemma 20.1(ii)) gives a·f = e·b. □
Theorem 21.2 (Reduced Form) Every ratio a : b has a unique reduced representative a0 :
b0 with no common stratifier k ≥ 2 (no k dividing both in the sense of Theorem 20.2 with zero
remainder), obtained by finitely many joint resolutions; a : b and a0 : b0 are the same ratio.

Proof If k ≥ 2 divides both — a = k·q1 , b = k·q2 exactly (Theorem 20.2, rem = 0) — then
a·q2 = k·q1 ·q2 = q1 ·b: a : b is the same ratio as q1 : q2 (Definition 21.2), with strictly smaller
terms (Lemma 20.1(i)). The counts are finite, so iterated joint resolution terminates at a
pair with no common stratifier. Uniqueness: two reduced representatives of one ratio satisfy
a0 ·d0 = c0 ·b0 ; the standard descent — any common structure forced across the equation
contradicts reducedness — is carried by Theorem 20.2’s uniqueness and Lemma 20.1, over
the finitely many counts below the given pair.
□
Theorem 21.3 (The Rational Projection, and the Gate Not Passed) The ratios under sameness, with the operations induced on representatives — (a:b) ⊞ (c:d) := (a·d + c·b) : (b·d) and
(a:b) ⊙ (c:d) := (a·c) : (b·d), where defined — project onto the positive rational arithmetic,
partial at the capacity bound. Every ratio is a relation between counts, hence enumerative
(Addendum 10.1): nothing in this section produces, presupposes, or opens continuous magnitude. What ratio establishes about the gate is its exact shape from below: ratio compares
accumulations that share the count substrate; a comparison between structures sharing no
common count — the incommensurable — is precisely what counts cannot express and what
any magnitude axioms would have to supply. The gate (F2) stands, now approached and
delimited, not passed. □
Remark (what ratio describes and enforces). Ratio describes relation between relations:
the second order of the framework’s own hierarchy, arrived at without new primitives. It
enforces completedness and co-assertion: only finished accumulations, jointly standing, compare — there is no ratio of the unresolved, and no ratio across the non-related. The chain

34

of domain conditions now reads: adjacency (sum), conjugacy (difference), stratified containment (product), stratification-in-fact (division), co-asserted completedness (ratio) — every
arithmetic operation bound to a relational condition, none free-floating. —

Experimental correspondence: Appendix B.

22 The Closure of F1: Coincidence of the Termini,
and the Residual Singleton
This section establishes the pacing of the descent and the residual singleton, closing
the solved state.
Dependencies. Axioms 2.2, 5.2 and 5.3 (including its discharge clause); Definitions 5.1,
5.2, 11.1 and 13.1; Propositions 3.1, 5.5, 5.4 and 14.1; Theorems 1, 4.8, 11.1, 11.2,
12.3; Corollary 5.11; Section 9 (bounded induction). Nothing else is used.
Definition 22.1 (Presentation) A unit is presented at a stratum when it stands enfolded
there in both orientations: the unit and its reversal both enfolded contents of that stratum
(Axiom 5.2). Presentation is the availability that lysis requires (Definition 13.1, Proposition 5.4): pairing acts only on presented units. Assertion is not presentation: a conjugate may
stand forced-asserted (Axiom 2.2, Proposition 3.1) without standing enfolded anywhere.
Theorem 22.1 (Presentation Is Generated Only by Allocation) A unit u_n — the boundary
allocated at depth n — becomes presented exactly at depth n + 1, and by no other mechanism.

Proof u_n is the mediation constituting S_n = S_n−1 ⊗ ¬S_n−1 (Proposition 5.5; at the
base, the envelope unit constitutes S0 ). Its reversal is rev(u_n) = ¬S_n−1 ⊗ S_n−1 =
¬S_n’s constituting boundary read in reverse orientation — concretely, by Theorem 12.1,
the reversal of the stratum is its opposite, and the stratum S_n+1 = S_n ⊗ ¬S_n enfolds
both S_n and ¬S_n (Axiom 5.2), thereby enfolding u_n in both orientations: u_n is presented at depth n + 1. No other mechanism enfolds: enfolding occurs only in the generation
of strata (Axiom 5.2 governs the partition mechanism of Proposition 5.5), constitutions
are immutable once generated (Proposition 14.1), and assertion without enfolding is not
presentation (Definition 22.1).
□
Theorem 22.2 (Exact Pacing) At every depth n with 1 ≤ n ≤ N: lysis has completed exactly
n pairings, and exactly one unit — the frontier u_n — is unpaired, its reversal not presented.

Proof Bounded induction (Theorem 10.2). At depth 1: the envelope unit u0 is presented
(Theorem 22.1), and lysis, deterministic on the eldest unpaired presented unit (Definition 13.1), pairs it: one pairing; the freshly allocated u1 is not presented (Theorem 22.1:
presentation waits for depth 2): one unpaired. Step: at depth n + 1 the previous frontier u_n
becomes presented (27.2) and is the unique eldest unpaired presented unit; lysis pairs it —
the (n+1)-th pairing; the new allocation u_n+1 is unpresented: exactly one unpaired. This

35

is Theorem 13.1’s bookkeeping with its mechanism now explicit: lysis keeps exact pace with
allocation, never ahead (the frontier is never presented at its own depth) and never behind
(determinism leaves no presented unit waiting).
□
Theorem 22.3 (Coincidence of the Termini, and the Residual Singleton) Lysis termination
and capacity exhaustion are one event — the harmonic point — and the closed structure
carries exactly one unpaired unit: the terminal frontier u_N, whose conjugate stands asserted
but never presented. Full pairing at closure is impossible.

Proof Allocation ceases at depth N (Theorem 5.10), and with it generation of strata ceases.
By Theorem 22.1, presentation is generated only by allocation: no unit unpresented at N is
ever presented. By Theorem 22.2, at depth N exactly one unit is unpaired — the terminal
frontier u_N — and it is unpresented. Lysis therefore has no admissible action at or beyond
the harmonic point (Definition 13.1 acts only on presented units): lysis terminates at exactly
the event of exhaustion, neither before (at every interior depth a presented eldest unit existed
and was paired, Theorem 22.2) nor after (nothing remains actionable). The two termini
coincide. The conjugate of u_N is forced-asserted: the representation of S_N forces ¬S_N
(Proposition 3.1), and rev(u_N) corresponds to ¬S_N (Theorem 12.1); but presentation of
u_N would require the stratum S_N+1 to enfold S_N with ¬S_N, and no such stratum
exists (Theorem 5.10) — the terminal demand is discharged by the envelope relation without
new allocation (Axiom 5.3, discharge clause), which supplies mediation, not enfolding. Hence
u_N is never paired by lysis — the residual singleton of the lysis ledger. Its mediation at
closure is nonetheless supplied: by the envelope relation, per Axiom 5.3’s discharge clause
and Theorem 22.4 below — pairing and mediation are distinct facts, and only the first is
absent. Full pairing by a successor stratum would require S_N+1, contradicting termination:
the withdrawn closure-pairing claim (F1) is not merely unproven but false. □
Remark (the structure of closure — corrected). The closed tower is N lysis pairings and
one terminal mediation carried by the highest relation (Theorem 22.4). The lysis ledger’s
unpaired count concerns lysis’s action domain — presented units — not mediation status:
every conjugate pair in the structure stands mediated, the terminal one by the envelope.
Closure is complete and admissible; what distinguishes the terminal unit is not that it is
unresolved but that its resolution runs through the origin. An earlier version of this remark
read the ledger fact as an open distinction; that reading contradicted Proposition 4.1 and
Axiom 5.3 and is withdrawn — the correction is recorded here per the method.
□
Theorem 22.4 (Reflection at Closure — the Between Closes by Satisfaction) At the harmonic point, the terminal conjugate pair stands at the minimal composite form — the very
form the envelope instantiates — and the mediation that Proposition 4.1 demands for it is
supplied by the envelope relation. The forcing of a new boundary lapses because its arising
condition is satisfied, not because the Between ceases: termination is the reflection of the
terminal state in the highest relation.

Proof (i) Every stratum is constituted by one and the same operation — a mediated conjugate
pair (Proposition 5.6, self-similarity); the terminal pair’s form is therefore the mediated-pair
form. (ii) At the harmonic point the finest admissible partition stands at the resolution floor:
the minimal composite form, below which no representable structure exists (Proposition 5.7,

36

Theorem 5.10, Axiom 5.3’s leveling clause — "the finest partition matches the boundary").
(iii) The envelope instantiates exactly that form at the origin (Proposition 5.7). (iv) Proposition 4.1’s demand is for a boundary to intercede between the terminal state and its opposite;
by Axiom 5.3’s discharge clause, at matching resolution that boundary is supplied by the
envelope relation itself. A new intercession is forced only where no matching mediation stands;
here one stands. (v) Hence the terminal pair is mediated — nothing stands unmediated anywhere in the closed structure, and admissibility (Axiom 2.1, Proposition 4.1) holds at closure
— while no new allocation occurs. The Between’s condition is met by the boundary that was
first in the order of being: bottom reflects top, and closure is satisfaction. □
Distinctness from the rejected identity claim. No identity of contents is asserted: S_N
̸= S0 , and enfolding asymmetry is intact (Axiom 5.2). What coincides is form at matching
resolution — reflection, not identity. The failure of the old termination-by-identity picture
and the validity of termination-by-reflection are compatible and now both established.
□

Experimental correspondence: Appendix C.

23 The Completeness Lemma: The Pre-Operator
Inventory Suffices for Decomposition
This section establishes the Completeness Lemma. (Classical background for the
standard instruments used here: [5, 6]; the derivations below are self-contained and
independent of these sources.)
Dependencies. the entire validated chain as itemized in the specification closure
computed below; principally Definitions 5.1, 13.1 and 22.1; Axioms 5.2 and 5.3; Propositions 5.5 and 14.1; Theorems 1, 4.8, 11.1–11.2, 12.3, 27.2–27.4, 27.6; Sections 9 and
23; Constructions 16.1, 16.2 and 16.1.
Definition 23.1 (The Descent Process) The descent process of an assertion is the pair
of mechanisms the chain defines, together with their termination and closure: allocation
— the partition mechanism generating one intercession per level (Axiom 5.3, Proposition 5.5) — and pairing — primitive lysis (Definition 13.1) acting on presented units
(Definition 22.1) — running to the harmonic point (Theorem 5.10), where both terminate in one event (Theorem 22.3) and the terminal mediation is carried by the envelope
(Theorem 22.4). Decomposition, in the theory’s core sequence — distinction, relation, solved
states, hierarchical lysis, fixed harmonic point — is this process.
Lemma 23.1 (Specification Closure) The complete specification of the descent process —
the citation closure of its defining mechanisms within this document — consists of exactly
fifty-four labeled items of the validated chain, together with Section 9’s counting structure
entering as a block, computed mechanically over the document’s citation graph from the
seeds Axiom 5.3, Proposition 5.5, Definition 13.1, Definition 22.1, Theorems 22.1, 22.2, 22.3
and 22.4: the Section 1 axioms and the primitive contradiction; representation and the costanding (2.1–2.2); coincidence and mediation (3.0–3.1); the full state architecture of Section
4 (boundary, partition orientation, interior conjugation, capacity, self-similarity, floor, finite
constituency, termination, harmonic point, chain); the domain items 5.1–5.3; the counting
structure of Section 9 with bounded induction; reversal and Theorem 1; opposition–reversal
coincidence and the unit flow (11.1–11.2); lysis and its ledger (12.1, 12.3); immutability

37

(13.1); Residence and the Determination Rule (16.1, 16.3); composition (20.3); the signedarithmetic items its bookkeeping uses (23.1–23.3, 23.6–23.8); and the closure results of Section
27. Every item of the closure stands proven in this document.

Proof The specification of a defined process is the dependency closure of its definitions and
governing theorems: what they cite, and what those citations cite, to the axioms. The closure was computed mechanically over the document’s citation graph from the stated seeds
and verified to contain only items proven herein; the computation is reproducible from the
document alone, and its item list is as stated. Sufficiency follows: every operation the process
performs — allocating, presenting, pairing, counting, terminating, closing — is governed by
a theorem in the closure, and every theorem in the closure is validated.
□
Lemma 23.2 (Gate-Freedom) The specification closure contains no excluded item: no
member of the magnitude gate (F2 — proportion, share, magnitude, weight), no refuted
closure-pairing item, and no open depth-bound item (F3). Decomposition is completely specified without magnitude. Nothing in the definitions of allocation, presentation, or pairing, nor
in their governing theorems, invokes a magnitude: the process’s data are contents, boundaries,
orientations, pairings, and counts — all upstream of the gate.
Theorem 23.3 (Completeness of the Pre-Operator Inventory) The validated chain is a
complete and gate-free specification of decomposition: every element required for the descent
process to run to its single closure stands proven (Lemma 23.1), and no required element
awaits the magnitude gate (Lemma 23.2). Consequently, under the ratified projection architecture — all quantitative structure derivable from the operator as projection — the operator’s
emergence is not blocked by any missing precondition: the tower it would fix is fully specified,
its decomposition fully governed, its closure proven single and admissible (Theorems 22.3 and
22.4).
Remark 23.1 (What This Lemma Does Not Claim) It does not derive the operator: emergence is the next and separate object, to be attempted from the higher Between under the
selection discipline — no axiom or step, direct or indirect, may select the operator; it must be
forced. It does not prove that magnitude is derivable from the operator: that demonstration
belongs after emergence, as the first projection. It establishes exactly the threshold condition: everything decomposition needs exists; nothing it needs is behind the gate; the way to
the emergence attempt is clear. —
