24 The Emergence Theorem: The Operator from the
Higher Between
This section derives the emergence of the operator from the constitution. (Classical
background for the standard instruments used here: [5, 6]; the derivations below are
self-contained and independent of these sources.)

38

Dependencies. Definitions 2.1, 3.1, 3.2, 5.1, 5.2, 6.1, 11.1, 13.1, 18.2 and 22.1;
Axioms 2.2, 2.4, 3.1, 4.1, 5.2 and 5.3; Propositions 2.1, 3.1, 4.1, 5.5, 5.6 and 14.1;
Theorems 1, 4.8, 11.1, 11.2, 12.3, 20.1, 20.2, 27.2–27.4, 27.6; Theorem 23.3. Nothing
else is used.
Theorem 24.1 (Stratification Is Mediation of Mediations) The tower’s ascent is the Between
applied to its own prior work: each stratum mediates a content whose constitution is already
relational. The higher Between is not a new mechanism; it is the tower itself, read at successive
constitutional depths.

Proof Each stratum is S_n+1 = S_n ⊗ ¬S_n (Proposition 5.5). Its terms are contents whose
constitutions comprise mediations (Definition 5.1, Axiom 5.2: the prior boundary and all prior
structure stand enfolded). The intercession generating S_n+1 therefore mediates relational
structure with its conjugate — mediation whose relata are constituted of mediations. By selfsimilarity (Proposition 5.6) this holds at every level: one operation, applied at each depth to
the product of its own prior applications.
□
Theorem 24.2 (Second-Order Mediation Is Forced) Every allocated unit, as an asserted
content, forces the co-standing with its conjugate and the mediation of that pair.

Proof An allocated unit u is a content (Definition 6.1: mediations are contents; Theorem 1
already quantifies over them) and stands asserted (allocation is assertion-driven, Axiom 5.3,
Definition 15.1). Its representation forces the co-standing R(u, ¬u) (Proposition 3.1,
Definition 3.2), and on allocation units the conjugate is the reversal, ¬u = rev u (Definition 18.2, Theorem 12.1). Unmediated co-standing of a term with its opposite is inadmissible
(Axiom 3.1, Proposition 2.1, Axiom 2.1); mediation of the pair is therefore forced (Proposition 4.1). This is the demand of the first order repeated one order up, by the same
citations.
□
Theorem 24.3 (Lysis Is the Discharge of the Second Order) The forced second-order
demands of a descent are discharged within the descent, with no new capacity: each interior
unit’s demand by its pairing, the terminal unit’s by the envelope.

Proof The pairing of u with rev u (Definition 13.1) converts the co-standing (u, rev u)
into a bound conjugate pair — a mediated second-order configuration, invariant as a pair
(Theorem 1(b)–(c)). Presentation (Definition 22.1) is exactly the adjacency condition under
which this intercession can act, and every interior unit is presented and paired in exact pace
with allocation (Theorems 22.1 and 22.2). The terminal unit’s demand is discharged by the
envelope relation at matching form (Theorem 22.4). Hence every second-order demand raised
by Theorem 24.2 is met by the descent’s own mechanisms; no stratum, unit, or capacity
beyond the genesis allocation is required (Axiom 5.3), and no infinite ascent of orders arises:
by Theorem 24.1 the orders are the levels, and the levels are fixed at genesis.
□

39

Definition 24.1 (The Second-Order Constitution) The second-order constitution of a solved
descent is the structure comprising: its allocated mediations; their adjacencies — containment
(co-constitution within a stratum, at radius one) and conjugacy (each with its reversal);
their orientations; and their pairings with the terminal envelope-discharge. Every component
is a constitutional fact of the descent already proven: the mediations by Axiom 5.3 and
Theorem 12.2; the adjacencies by Axiom 5.2 and Theorem 16.2; the orientations by Axiom 2.4;
the pairings by Definition 13.1 and Theorems 22.2 and 22.4. No component is new, and no
component is quantitative.
Theorem 24.4 (Emergence: the Second-Order Constitution Is Forced and Unique) The
second-order constitution of every solved descent is completely determined by the chain, with
no choice at any point: it emerges.

Proof Each component is forced. The demands: which pairs must be mediated is fixed by
Theorem 24.2 — every allocated unit with its reversal, no more, no fewer. The discharges:
which intercession meets each demand is fixed by determinism and pacing (Definition 13.1’s
eldest-first rule, Theorem 22.2) and, at the terminus, by Theorem 22.4 — a unique discharge
assignment. The adjacencies: primitive operations have radius one (Theorem 16.2) — adjacency is not a menu but a theorem. The intercessions: each allocates identically (Axiom 5.3)
— uniform, with no second act available to the Between. The symmetry: each discharged
pair is invariant as a pair under reversal (Theorem 1(c)). The covariance: every component
is a constitutional fact, and constitution carries no labels (Theorem 16.1). Uniqueness: suppose two structures each satisfy all forced conditions on the same descent. Their demand sets
are identical (forced), their discharge assignments identical (deterministic), their adjacencies
identical (theorem, not choice), their intercessions identical (uniform), their orientations identical (the descent’s own, Axiom 2.4). Structures with identical constitutions are identical —
identity is constitution (Definition 5.1). Hence exactly one structure satisfies the forced conditions, and it is the descent’s own constitution read at second order. Nothing was selected:
every clause above cites a forcing result, and at no point did any clause distinguish among
candidates, because no candidate set exists — the structure is not chosen from possibilities
but read off necessities.
□
Remark 24.1 (The Operator as the Rendering of Forced Structure) Scoped statement,
downstream-facing by its vocabulary: in any rendering that assigns values to contents and
expresses comparisons as magnitudes — rendering being, by definition, downstream of the
gate — the unique structure of Theorem 24.4 renders as a comparison structure that is
uniform across intercessions, symmetric under conjugation, local at radius one, and labelcovariant. That profile is the defining profile of the covariant self-adjoint operator of the
downstream formulation: self-adjointness as the rendering of pair-symmetry, covariance as
the rendering of label-freedom, locality as the rendering of radius one, uniformity as the rendering of the Between’s single act — with the weights as the rendering of the securitizer’s
office (Definition 17.1, Theorems 17.1 and 17.2), whose datum awaits the projection program.
The operator thereby emerges as the rendering of forced structure. One further correspondence is recorded as observation: the fully aligned totality at closure — every pair bound,
the terminal mediation carried by the envelope — renders as the structure’s invariant direction, the rendering-level kernel. No magnitude is asserted, computed, or presupposed in this
observation.

40

Experimental correspondence: Appendix C.

25 The First Projection: Magnitude from the
Operator
This section derives the sector equation and the binding constant. (Classical background for the standard instruments used here: [3]; the derivations below are
self-contained and independent of these sources.)
Dependencies. Theorem 1; Definitions 21.1, 21.2 and 24.1; Lemmas 18.4 and 20.1;
Theorems 10.2, 17.1, 17.2, 18.3, 18.6, 19.2, 20.2, 21.1, 21.2, 21.3 and 24.4; Corollaries
4.9, 16.6; Sections 9 and 23 (count substrate). Nothing else is used.
Definition 25.1 (Rendering) A rendering of the second-order constitution (Definition 24.1,
Theorem 24.4) is any downstream expression of it that is consistent with what it renders:
on count-expressible content, a rendering restricts to the validated count and ratio structure
(Sections 9, 23–26) — its comparisons agree with ratios where ratios exist, its combinations
with the sum and product laws (Theorems 18.3, 18.6, 19.2 and 21.3). This is not an axiom
of the primitive chain but the definition of rendering: to render a structure is to extend the
structure’s own projections coherently. Renderings are downstream objects by definition; the
primitive chain asserts nothing about magnitude, and the gate of Section 26 stands untouched.
Theorem 25.1 (The Forced Sector Equation) In every consistent rendering, the solved
state’s sectors bear shares (d, s) — aligned and residual as fractions of the whole — satisfying
s ◦ s ⊞ s = unity, rendered: s2 + s = 1
at every level, with one dimensionless datum throughout.

Proof Each clause quantifies over all consistent renderings and cites a forcing. Unity: the
whole compared with itself is the reflexive ratio (Theorem 21.1), forced, not a normalization
choice. Additivity: the sectors are exclusive and jointly exhaustive with disjoint contents
(Theorem 1(c)); a consistent rendering combines disjoint exhaustive contents as the count
laws do (Theorems 18.3 and 18.6: term-additivity over disjoint constitutions), so d ⊞ s =
unity. Composition: the aligned sector is constituted exactly by conjugate pairs of residual
content (Theorem 1(b)–(c)) — residual content taken through one level of containment; a
consistent rendering composes shares through containment as counting does (Theorem 19.2:
multiplication is counting through containment; Theorem 21.3: the induced ratio product),
so d = s ◦ s. Constancy and dimensionlessness: the datum is the securitizer’s, constant
across pairs and levels on pain of regress (Theorem 17.1) and dimensionless (Theorem 17.2).
Substituting: s2 + s = 1 in every consistent rendering. No rendering was chosen; the equation
is what all of them share.
□
Theorem 25.2 (Incommensurability of the Datum) No ratio satisfies the sector equation:
for no counts p, q ≥ 1 does (p : q) ◦ (p : q) ⊞ (p : q) equal unity. The securitizer’s datum is
not count-expressible.

41

Proof Suppose p : q reduced (Theorem 21.2) with (p2 : q2 ) ⊞ (p : q) = 1 : 1. By the
induced operations (Theorem 21.3) and the cross criterion (Definition 21.2) with cancellation
(Lemma 20.1(ii)), this is p2 + p·q = q2 . First, p < q: if p ≥ q then p2 + pq ≥ 2q2 > q2 . Now
descend: set p′ = q − p and q′ = p (both ≥ 1; subtraction by Lemma 18.4, Theorem 18.5).
Then p′2 + p′ q′ = (q − p)2 + (q − p)p = q2 − pq = p2 = q′2 , using the supposed
equation at the last step: (p′ , q′ ) satisfies the same equation with q′ = p < q, strictly smaller.
Iterating produces an infinite strictly descending chain of counts, impossible on the finite
chain (Corollary 5.11, Theorem 10.2). No solution exists. □
Remark. This confirms the gate of Section 26 from above: the first genuinely magnitudelevel object of the framework — the securitizer’s datum — is exactly a comparison no ratio
expresses, existing only in renderings. The gate was not a defect of the chain but a theorem
about where magnitude lives.
□
Theorem 25.3 (The Convergents and the Unit Defect) Define counts by the recursion c1
= 1, c2 = 1, c_k+2 = c_k+1 + c_k (Section 9, within capacity). Then at every order k,
c_k2 + c_k·c_k+1 − c_k+12 = ±1, alternating in orientation
: the reduced ratios c_k : c_k+1 miss the sector equation by exactly one count — the
minimal possible defect — with the sign alternating at each order.

Proof Bounded induction (Theorem 10.2). Base: 1 + 1 − 1 = +1. Step: writing d_k for the
defect and using c_k+2 = c_k+1 + c_k, direct count algebra gives d_k+1 = c_k+12 +
c_k+1c_k+2 − c_k+22 = c_k+12 − c_k+1c_k − c_k2 = −d_k. So the defect is ±1
forever, alternating; it is never 0 (Theorem 25.2), and one is the least nonzero count. The
consecutive pairs are reduced: a common stratifier of c_k and c_k+1 would stratify the defect
±1 (Theorem 20.2), forcing it to be 1. The datum is thereby bracketed by count structure to
within a single count at every order, from alternating sides — the tightest expression counts
can give of what they cannot express.
□

Experimental correspondence: Appendix C.

26 Position, Distance, and the Closure of F3: The
Relational Metric
This section establishes position, Distance, and the Closure of F3.
Dependencies. Axioms 2.3, 2.4, 5.2 and 5.3; Definitions 5.1, 11.1, 13.1 and 24.1; Propositions 5.5, 5.9 and 14.1; Theorems 1, 9.4, 11.1, 16.1, 20.2, 23.5, 27.4; Corollaries 4.9,
7.3, 16.5; Rule 15.2; Construction 16.1; Sections 9, 23. Nothing else is used.
Definition 26.1 (Position) The position of a content within a constitution is its address: the
oriented path-word of its residence (Theorem 15.1, Theorem 15.3). Position is constitutionpath — a structural fact, unique per content, requiring no space to be defined in: the
constitution is the space.
Definition 26.2 (Adjacency) Contents a, b are adjacent when they stand in immediate
constitution: either one immediately enfolds the other (container and constituent, Axiom 5.2),
or they are co-constituents of one mediation (the two sectors separated by a single boundary,

42

Definition 5.1). Adjacency is symmetric by constitution — enfolding relates both relata, and
co-constituency is mutual — and it is exactly the radius-one standing of Theorem 16.2 and
Definition 24.1: no primitive relation reaches farther.
Definition 26.3 (Passage, and the Domain Condition of Geometry) A passage from a to
b is a finite sequence of contents, beginning at a and ending at b, each adjacent to the
next; its length is the count of its steps. A passage is descriptive — a reading of standing
relations — not an operation on the tower: nothing is generated or altered (Proposition 14.1 is
untouched). Passages exist only within a common constitution: contents sharing no enfolding
ancestry stand in no adjacency chain, and no passage joins them. Distance, like the sum
(Definition 18.1), carries a relational domain condition: there is no metric across the
non-related. Geometry does not precede relation; it is read off relation.
Lemma 26.1 (Least Count) Every nonempty collection of counts within the bound has a
least member.

Proof Bounded induction (Theorem 10.2) on the chain: if 0 belongs to the collection it is
least (nothing precedes it, Theorem 10.2); otherwise pass to the successor stage and apply
the hypothesis to the collection’s members beyond it; the chain is finite (Corollary 5.11), so
the process terminates at the first member reached.
□
Theorem 26.2 (Connectedness of a Constitution) Any two contents of one constitution are
joined by a passage.

Proof Every content of the constitution is reached from the whole by a chain of immediateconstituent steps (Axiom 5.2, finite by Proposition 5.9). Reversing one such chain and
following another joins any two contents through their common container: a passage.
□
Theorem 26.3 (The Relational Metric) On the contents of one constitution, let d(a, b) be
the least length of a passage from a to b (existing by Theorem 26.2 and Lemma 26.1). Then
d is a metric: (i) d(a, b) = 0 exactly when a = b; (ii) d(a, b) = d(b, a); (iii) d(a, c) ≤ d(a,
b) + d(b, c).

Proof (i) The empty passage joins a content to itself, so d(a, a) = 0; a passage of length 0 has
coinciding endpoints, so d(a, b) = 0 forces a = b. (ii) Adjacency is symmetric (Definition 26.2),
so the reversal of a passage is a passage of equal length; least lengths coincide. (iii) The
concatenation of a passage from a to b with one from b to c is a passage from a to c whose
length is the sum of the lengths (counts of disjoint step-collections add, Section 9; the shared
endpoint contributes no step); the least length is at most this sum.
□
Theorem 26.4 (The Valence Profile) Within the constitution of a closed descent of depth N,
the adjacency count of each content is fully determined, and it is not uniform: interior strata
and interior conjugates have valence five — two enfolding adjacencies (each is a constituent

43

of both members of the next conjugate pair), two constitutional adjacencies (its own sectors),
and one lateral adjacency (its co-constituent conjugate); the bare terms have valence three;
the penultimate stratum has valence four; and the terminal stratum has valence two.

Proof By the enfolding facts alone. An interior stratum S_n (n ≤ N − 2) is a constituent
of both S_n+1 and ¬S_n+1 — the latter enfolds it because ¬S_n+1 = ¬S_n ⊗ S_n
(Axiom 2.3, Theorem 12.1) — giving two upward adjacencies; its own constituents S_n−1,
¬S_n−1 give two downward; its co-constituent ¬S_n gives one lateral: five, and the same
count holds for interior conjugates by the same computation. The terms x, ¬x are constituents
of S0 and of ¬S0 (two upward) and co-constituents of each other (one lateral): three. S_N−1
loses one upward adjacency — ¬S_N is asserted but never enfolded (Theorem 22.3) — leaving
four. The terminal stratum S_N has its two constituents and nothing above or beside it
within the constitution: two. □
Remark (valence is not dimension). The valence profile is a count of standing adjacencies
— a theorem of enfolding — and licenses no claim about dimensionality, which is a renderinglevel notion not defined in this chain. What the profile does exhibit, structurally, is closure
itself: the terminal stratum’s reduced valence is the geometric signature of Theorem 22.3 —
the descent’s end is visible in its adjacency structure.
□
Lemma 26.5 (Conjugate Height) Define the height h of a content as the greatest length of
an enfolding chain from it to a bare term (existing by Proposition 5.9 and Lemma 26.1 applied
to the finite collection of chain lengths, greatest by the dual argument). Then h(¬a) = h(a).

Proof Bounded induction on constitution: opposition acts component-wise (Axiom 2.3), so
¬a’s constituents are the opposites of a’s constituents, and its chains correspond one-to-one
with a’s chains, length for length.
□
Theorem 26.6 (The Composite Height Law — F3 Closed) For any composite of distinct
co-asserted contents,
h(a ◦ b) = 1 + max(h(a), h(b))
exactly. Consequently h(a ◦ b) ≤ h(a) + h(b) + 1 holds a fortiori — the bound the former
F3 sought — and, with the relation-count additivity already proven (Theorem 18.3), both
structural measures of composition are now theorems: F3 is closed.

Proof The immediate constituents of a ◦ b = a ⊗ b are a and b, each enfolded whole,
and the mediating boundary is a relation, not a content-step (Definition 5.1, Axiom 5.2,
Construction 16.1). Every enfolding chain from the composite therefore passes first to a or
to b and continues as a chain of that constituent: its length is one more than a chain of a or
of b, and the greatest such is 1 + max(h(a), h(b)). Consistency with the descent: h(S_n) =
n + 1 by this law with Lemma 26.5 (each stratum’s two constituents have equal height), so
constitutional height exceeds the stratum count of Rule 15.2 by exactly one — two counters,
explicitly related, in no conflict.
□

Experimental correspondence: Appendix D.

44

27 The Angular Structure and the Origin of Odd
Circuits
This section establishes the Angular Structure and the Origin of Odd Circuits.
Dependencies. Axioms 2.3 and 5.2; Definitions 5.1, 25.1, 26.1, 26.2 and 26.3; Theorems
1, 11.1, 20.2, 22.2, 22.3, 23.5, 27.4, 31.5–31.8 with Lemmas 26.1 and 26.5; Sections 9,
23. Nothing else is used.
Definition 27.1 (Bearings and the Separation Profile) A bearing pair at a content a is a
pair of distinct adjacencies of a — two departures u, v from the same content. Its separation
profile is the triple of distances (d(a,u), d(a,v), d(u,v)) = (1, 1, d(u,v)). A bearing pair is
closed when its departures are themselves adjacent, open otherwise.
Theorem 27.1 (The Binary Angular Structure at Radius One) Every bearing pair has
separation profile (1, 1, 1) or (1, 1, 2), and both occur: the primitive angular structure is
exactly binary.

Proof u and v are each adjacent to a, so the passage u–a–v gives d(u,v) ≤ 2 (Theorem 26.3);
d(u,v) = 0 is excluded since u ̸= v (Theorem 26.3(i)); d(u,v) = 1 exactly when u, v are
adjacent. Both cases are realized (Theorem 27.2). What angle describes about relation, at the
primitive level, is precisely this: whether two departures from a common content themselves
stand in relation.
□
Theorem 27.2 (The Bearing Classification at Interior Contents) At an interior stratum or
interior conjugate (valence five, Theorem 26.4), the ten bearing pairs split as exactly six closed
and four open, and the open bearings are precisely the enfolding-crossings — one departure
upward, one downward. The lateral (conjugate) departure is closed with every other departure.

Proof By the enfolding facts, as in Theorem 26.4. The two upward neighbors are coconstituents of the next stratum: adjacent. The two downward neighbors are co-constituents
of the content itself: adjacent. The lateral neighbor ¬S_n is an immediate constituent of
both upward neighbors and an immediate container of both downward neighbors (Axiom 2.3,
Theorem 12.1): adjacent to all four. The four up-down pairs are non-adjacent: an upward
neighbor’s immediate constituents are the content and its conjugate, not the content’s constituents. Count: closed = 1 (up-up) + 1 (down-down) + 4 (lateral with each) = 6; open
= 4 (the crossings). computationally verified on the assembled constitution graph at every
interior content.
□
Theorem 27.3 (The Rendering-Forced Angular Dichotomy) In every consistent rendering
(Definition 25.1): open bearings are straight — the rendered separation of the departures
equals the sum of the rendered lengths of the two steps — and closed bearings are strictly
bent — the rendered separation is strictly less than that sum. Moreover, the uniform binding

45

(Theorems 17.1 and 17.2) scales every step by the one dimensionless datum, so all rendered distance ratios, and with them the entire angular classification, are rendering-invariant;
absolute rendered lengths are not.

Proof Consistency restricts a rendering to the count and ratio structure on count-expressible
content (Definition 25.1): the count identities 2 = 1 + 1 and 1 < 2 are preserved as rendered
equality and strict order. For an open bearing, d(u,v) = 2 with the two-step passage through
a realizing it: rendered separation = sum — the equality case of the triangle law, tight,
hence straight. For a closed bearing, d(u,v) = 1 < 2: strictly less — bent. Uniformity: every
adjacency carries the securitizer’s single datum (Theorem 17.1), so every rendered length is
the step count composed with one scale; ratios cancel it (dimensionlessness, Theorem 17.2).
Through every interior content, the four straight bearings are exactly the enfolding-crossings
(Theorem 27.2): the depth direction renders as geodesic straightness, and bending occurs
only through relations that close triangles.
□
Lemma 27.4 (The Height-Step Law) Along any adjacency, height changes by exactly one
for an enfolding adjacency and by zero for a co-constituent (lateral) adjacency.

Proof Immediate constitution raises height by one (Theorem 26.6’s chain construction); coconstituents of one mediation have equal height (Lemma 26.5: conjugates have equal height,
and the sectors of one boundary are conjugate, Theorem 12.1). computationally verified over
every edge of the assembled graph.
□
Theorem 27.5 (Conjugation Is the Sole Source of Odd Circuits) Every circuit of odd length
traverses at least one lateral (conjugate co-constituent) adjacency. The enfolding structure
alone contains no odd circuit — it is two-colorable by height parity — though it is not circuitfree: the double-containment diamond, S_n−1 — S_n — ¬S_n−1 — ¬S_n — S_n−1, is
an even circuit of enfolding adjacencies alone. In particular every triangle — every closed
bearing — passes through conjugation.

Proof Around any circuit the height changes sum to zero. Enfolding steps contribute ±1 and
lateral steps 0 (Lemma 27.4), so the number of enfolding steps in any circuit is even. A circuit
of odd length therefore contains an odd — hence nonzero — number of lateral steps. The
diamond is exhibited: each of its four edges is an immediate-constitution adjacency (S_n−1
and ¬S_n−1 are each constituents of both S_n and ¬S_n, Axiom 2.3, Theorem 12.1), and
its length is even, consistent with the parity law. The triangle claim is the odd case at length
three, and the computation census over the assembled graph confirms it at every triangle. □
Remark (what this locates). Circuit structure is the precondition of curvature in any
rendering: where there are no circuits there is nothing for transport to disagree around.
The theorem locates the origin exactly: odd circulation — the triangle content that makes
bearings close — exists only through conjugation. Enfolding alone is parity-flat. No curvature
magnitude is asserted; the location of its possibility is proven.
□

Experimental correspondence: Appendix D.

46

28 The Primitive Ancestor of Curvature: The Unit
Circuit Defect and the Triangle Census
This section establishes the Primitive Ancestor of Curvature. (Classical background
for the standard instruments used here: [7]; the derivations below are self-contained
and independent of these sources.)
Dependencies. Axioms 2.3 and 5.2; Definitions 5.1, 25.1, 26.2, 26.3 and 27.1; Theorems
1, 11.1, 22.2, 22.3, 27.4, 31.6, 31.7, 32.2, 32.3, 32.5 with Lemma 27.4; Sections 9, 23.
Nothing else is used.
Definition 28.1 (Circuit Defect) The defect of a closed bearing — a triangle a, u, v — is the
count by which the direct passage undercuts the detour: the length of the two-step passage
through the apex minus the length of the direct step, 2 − 1 = 1 as counts (Theorem 27.1).
Defect is the primitive quantity of bending: how much relation the closure saves.
Theorem 28.1 (The Unit Defect, and Its Rendering) Every minimal circuit has defect
exactly one. In every consistent rendering, the defect of every minimal circuit renders as
exactly one binding — the securitizer’s single dimensionless datum — uniform across all
minimal circuits of all descents.

Proof Minimal circuits are triangles (a circuit has length at least three, and triangles
exist, Theorem 27.2), and every triangle’s separation profile is (1, 1, 1) with detour 2
(Theorem 27.1): defect 2 − 1 = 1, a count identity. A consistent rendering preserves count
identities and renders each step as one binding bearing the one datum (Definition 25.1; Theorems 17.1 and 17.2): the rendered defect is one rendered step — the datum — for every
minimal circuit alike, in every rendering, with no dependence on position, depth, or descent.
Bending, at its origin, is quantized, and its quantum is the securitizer’s datum.
□
Lemma 28.2 (Exactly One Lateral per Triangle) Every triangle traverses exactly one lateral
adjacency.

Proof At least one, by odd-circuit exclusivity (Theorem 27.5); the count of laterals in a
triangle is odd (the parity argument of 32.5: the enfolding steps number even, here 3 − #lat,
so #lat is odd), hence one or three. Exactly one.
□
Theorem 28.3 (The Triangle Census) The constitution of a closed descent of depth N
contains exactly
4N + 1
triangles, distributed by lateral edge: two at the term pair, four at each of the laterals of
levels 0 through N − 2, and three at the terminal lateral of level N − 1.

Proof By Lemma 28.2, triangles are counted by their unique lateral edge; a triangle on the
lateral p, ¬p is completed by any content adjacent to both members. For an interior lateral at

47

level n (1 ≤ n ≤ N − 2): both containers S_n+1, ¬S_n+1 enfold both members (Axiom 2.3,
Theorem 12.1), and both constituents S_n−1, ¬S_n−1 are enfolded by both members —
four completions. The level-0 lateral has the two containers and the two terms: four. The
term pair has only its two containers S0 , ¬S0 : two. The terminal lateral loses one container —
¬S_N is asserted but never enfolded (Theorem 22.3) — leaving S_N and its two constituents:
three. Total: 2 + 4 + 4(N − 2) + 3 = 4N + 1. computationally verified exactly, with the full
distribution, at every depth from four through ten.
□
Theorem 28.4 (The Defect Distribution, and Closure’s Second Signature) The total primitive defect of a closed descent is 4N + 1 — one per triangle — rendering in every consistent
rendering as 4N + 1 bindings of the one datum; the defect density is four per interior level,
uniform; and the terminal lateral’s deficit — three where the interior carries four — is the
second geometric signature of closure: Theorem 22.3, already visible in the valence profile
(Theorem 26.4), reappears in the distribution of bending. The two boundary readings agree:
what closes the descent thins both adjacency and circulation at the top.

Experimental correspondence: Appendix D.

29 The Composition of Circulation: Triangles
Generate All Circuits
This section establishes the Composition of Circulation. (Classical background for
the standard instruments used here: [7]; the derivations below are self-contained and
independent of these sources.)
Dependencies. Axioms 2.3 and 5.2; Definitions 5.1, 25.1, 26.2, 26.3, 27.1 and 28.1;
Theorems 1, 11.1, 22.2, 27.4, 31.7, 32.2, 32.3, 32.5, 33.2–33.4 with Lemmas 27.4 and
28.2; Sections 9, 23. Nothing else is used.
Definition 29.1 (Composition of Circuits) The composition of two circuit configurations is
the configuration of adjacencies traversed by exactly one of the two. Composition is associative
and self-inverse: composing a configuration with itself leaves nothing.
Lemma 29.1 (Circuit Decomposition) Any composition of circuits is a disjoint union of
circuits.

Proof In the composed configuration every content meets an even count of traversed adjacencies: each constituent circuit contributes an even count at each content (a circuit enters as
often as it leaves), and composition removes doubly-traversed adjacencies in pairs. From any
traversed adjacency, follow traversed adjacencies — always possible at even degree — until a
content repeats (finiteness, Proposition 5.9, Corollary 5.11): a circuit; remove it, preserving
evenness; repeat until nothing remains (Theorem 10.2).
□

48

Theorem 29.2 (The Circulation Rank) The independent circulation of a closed descent of
depth N — the greatest count of circuits none of which is a composition of the others — is
exactly
3N + 1

Proof By induction on depth (Theorem 10.2). At each ascent from depth N to N + 1, the
constitution gains two contents (the new stratum and the newly enfolded conjugate) and five
adjacencies (the new stratum’s two constituents, the enfolded conjugate’s two constituents,
and the new lateral): the circulation count grows by exactly 5 − 2 = 3, since each new
adjacency beyond a spanning skeleton of the new contents contributes one independent circuit
and a skeleton needs two of the five. The base is verified directly, and the closed form 3N +
1 follows; computationally verified exactly at every depth from four through nine (13, 16, 19,
22, 25, 28).
□
Theorem 29.3 (Diamond Resolution) The enfolding diamond — the even circuit S_n−1
— S_n — ¬S_n−1 — ¬S_n — S_n−1 of Theorem 27.5 — is the composition of the two
triangles that share its lateral chord: S_n−1, ¬S_n−1, S_n composed with S_n−1, ¬S_n−1,
¬S_n. The shared lateral adjacency cancels; the diamond’s four enfolding adjacencies remain.

Proof Direct exhibit, adjacency by adjacency (Axiom 2.3, Theorem 12.1 supplying the
enfoldings); computationally verified. Even circulation, which conjugation does not traverse,
is nonetheless resolved by conjugation: the lateral chord mediates the diamond into two
unit-defect cells.
□
Theorem 29.4 (Triangles Generate All Circulation) Every circuit of the constitution is a
composition of triangles.

Proof It suffices that the triangles’ independent count equals the circulation rank. By induction on depth with a private-adjacency argument: at each ascent, among the four new triangles
(Theorem 28.3’s accounting — the three on the new lateral, and the fourth completing the
previous lateral), the container triangle is the only circuit yet in existence traversing the new
stratum’s adjacencies, and each of the two constituent triangles is the only one traversing
its own new constituent adjacency: three of the new triangles each hold an adjacency private to themselves among all circuits so far, hence are independent of everything prior and
of each other — the circulation count’s growth of three (Theorem 29.2) is met exactly. The
base is verified directly. Therefore the triangles’ independent count equals 3N + 1 at every
depth, and every circuit is a composition of triangles (Lemma 29.1 closing the composition).
computationally verified in full: at every depth from four through nine, the triangle set’s
independent count equals the circulation rank exactly.
□
Corollary 29.5 (The Defect Accounting) Every circuit’s bending is accounted by unit-defect
cells: a circuit composed of k triangles carries their composed defect, and in every consistent rendering (Definition 25.1) all circulation everywhere in the constitution is composed of

49

quanta of the one datum (Theorems 28.1 and 17.1). There is no circulation that the minimal
cells do not generate, and no bending that is not built of the securitizer’s quantum.

Experimental correspondence: Appendix D.

30 Dimension, First Step: The Linearity of the
Descent
This section establishes dimension, First Step. (Classical background for the standard
instruments used here: [7]; the derivations below are self-contained and independent
of these sources.)
Dependencies. Axioms 2.3 and 5.2; Definitions 5.1, 26.1, 26.2 and 26.3; Propositions 5.9
and 14.1; Theorems 1, 9.4, 11.1, 27.4, 31.5–31.8 with Lemmas 26.1, 26.5 and 27.4;
Corollary 5.11; Sections 9, 23. Nothing else is used.
Definition 30.1 (Balls and Growth) For a content a and count r, the ball B(a, r) is the
collection of contents at distance at most r from a (Theorem 26.3), with |B(a, r)| its count
(finite, Proposition 5.9). Growth is compared by count inequalities: a structure has linear
growth when counts α, β exist with α·r ≤ |B(a, r)| ≤ β·r + β for all admissible r — bracketing
by multiples, entirely within bounded arithmetic.
Theorem 30.1 (Depth Is Distance) On the strata, metric distance equals height difference
exactly: d(S_m, S_n) = |m − n|, and likewise d(¬S_m, ¬S_n) = |m − n|; across the two
spines, d(S_m, ¬S_n) = |m − n| for m ̸= n and 1 for m = n.

Proof Upper bounds: the enfolding chain S_m — S_m+1 — · · · — S_n is a passage of
length |m − n|; for the cross case, the same chain followed by the lateral step, or the enfolding
path through the shared container (each stratum and its conjugate are co-constituents at
the next level, adjacent to a common container), realizing |m − n| when heights differ and
1 when equal. Lower bounds: every adjacency changes height by at most one (Lemma 27.4),
so any passage between contents of heights h1 , h2 has length at least |h1 − h2 |; for equal
heights and distinct contents, at least one step. Equality throughout; computationally verified
exhaustively at depth twenty. Depth, the tower’s own stratification count, is thereby literal
metric distance: the descent’s order of resolution is its geometry.
□
Theorem 30.2 (Exact Linear Growth) For an interior content a of height h with r ≤ min(h
− 1, N − h):
|B(a, r)| = 4r + 2
exactly; at every content and radius, 2r + 1 ≤ |B(a, r)| ≤ 4r + 2. The descent has linear
growth in the exact sense of Definition 30.1.

Proof Heights reachable within r steps are exactly h − r through h + r (Lemma 27.4 for
the bound; enfolding chains for the attainment), a count of 2r + 1 heights. Within range,

50

every height carries exactly two contents — the stratum and its conjugate (the terms at
height zero likewise two) — each reachable within the height difference, and the pair at each
height within one further lateral step, all within r by the range condition: the ball is the full
double-spine segment, 2(2r + 1) = 4r + 2. The universal upper bound is the same counting
without the attainment claim; the lower bound counts one content per reachable height along
the surviving directions at the boundaries. computationally verified exactly: interior balls at
depth twenty give 6, 10, 14, 18, 22, 26 at radii one through six — 4r + 2 without exception. □
Corollary 30.3 (One-Dimensionality of the Single Descent, and the Relocation of Dimension) A single descent is one-dimensional in the growth sense: its balls grow linearly, exactly,
and no single-descent structure exceeds linear growth — the upper bound of Theorem 30.2
is universal over its contents. Consequently, dimension beyond one is not a property of the
descent process at all: whatever carries higher-dimensional growth must be composition across
descents — the plurality of assertions in relation (Remark 15.1, Construction 16.1). Dimension is hereby relocated, by proof: from the tower to the composed manifold of towers. What
the composed structure’s growth is, and whether any count of composition directions is forced,
is the next object; nothing about its value is claimed here.

Experimental correspondence: Appendix D.

31 Dimension, Second Step: The Growth of
Composed Pluralities
This section establishes dimension, Second Step. (Classical background for the
standard instruments used here: [7]; the derivations below are self-contained and
independent of these sources.)
Dependencies. Construction 16.1; Definitions 5.1, 26.1, 26.2, 26.3 and 30.1; Axiom 5.2;
Propositions 5.9 and 14.1; Theorems 1, 9.4, 23.5, 31.5–31.8, 35.2–35.3 with Corollary 30.3 and Lemmas 26.1, 26.5 and 27.4; Remark 15.1; Rule 15.2; Sections 9, 23.
Nothing else is used.
Definition 31.1 (Sequential and Nested Composition) The sequential composite of descents
a1 , . . . , a_K is the left-iterated composition c_K = (· · · (a1 ◦ a2 ) ◦ · · · ) ◦ a_K (Construction 16.1): its constitution is a chain of composite mediations — junctions — each enfolding
the previous composite and the next descent whole, the descent entered at its terminal
stratum. Nested sequencing iterates the construction: a level-d structure is the sequential
composite of level-(d − 1) structures, with level-1 the single descent. All are contents of the
chain, residing and composable by the validated constructions; all counts are within capacity,
inheriting Section 9’s partiality.
Theorem 31.1 (Quadratic Growth of the Sequential Composite) In the sequential composite, balls centered at interior junctions satisfy, for admissible r (at least four, and within the
junction chain and descent depths):
r2 /4 ≤ |B(r)| ≤ 9r2
— quadratic growth exactly, in the sense of Definition 30.1 extended to degree two.

51

Proof Upper bound: within radius r of a junction lie at most 2r + 1 junctions (the junction
chain has unit steps), and through each, entry into at most one pendant descent explored
to depth at most r, contributing at most 2r + 2 contents (Theorem 30.2’s per-height census
entered at the terminal stratum): |B(r)| ≤ (2r + 1)(2r + 3) ≤ 9r2 . Lower bound: the junctions
at steps 1 through r − 1 on one side each admit entry into a distinct pendant, and the pendant
reached after s steps contributes at least r − s − 1 contents along its spine (Theorem 30.1’s
exact spine distances): |B(r)| ≥ Σ_s=1^r−1(r − s − 1) = (r − 1)(r − 2)/2 ≥ r2 /4 for r ≥
4. computationally verified on the assembled composite (thirty descents of depth thirty): the
measured balls sit within both bounds at every tested radius, with growth exponent fitting
1.95.
□
Theorem 31.2 (Every Degree Is Attained) For every d ≥ 1 within capacity there exist
counts κ_d ≤ 8^d and λ_d ≤ 6^d and a threshold r_d such that the level-d nested composite
satisfies
r^d ≤ κ_d · |B_d(r)| and |B_d(r)| ≤ λ_d · r^d
for all admissible r ≥ r_d: nested sequencing attains every polynomial growth degree.

Proof Induction on d (Theorem 10.2). Level one is Theorem 30.2 (κ1 = 4, λ1 = 6). Step:
a level-d ball at an interior junction contains, on one side, the junctions at steps 1 through
⌊r/2⌋, each entering a distinct level-(d − 1) pendant explored to radius at least ⌊r/2⌋ − s,
contributing at least (⌊r/2⌋ − s)^d−1/κ_d−1 by hypothesis; the sum exceeds r^d/(2^d+1
κ_d−1) beyond a threshold absorbing the integer slack, giving κ_d ≤ 2^d+1 κ_d−1 ≤
8^d. Upward: at most 2r + 1 junctions, each contributing at most 1 + λ_d−1 r^d−1:
|B_d(r)| ≤ 3r · 2λ_d−1 r^d−1 = 6 λ_d−1 r^d beyond the threshold, giving λ_d ≤ 6^d.
computationally verified at levels two and three: exponent fits 1.95 and 2.84 on assembled
structures, within the constructed bounds.
□
Corollary 31.3 (Dimension Is Assertional) Growth class is a property of the asserted composition structure, not of the descent process: a single assertion is a line (Corollary 30.3);
a sequential plurality carries quadratic growth (36.2); nesting ascends through every degree
(36.3). By the universal/per-assertion split (Rule 15.2, Corollary 15.4), the availability of
every degree is universal — proven here by construction — while the degree any world exhibits
is fixed by what that world’s assertions compose: dimension is read from the plurality’s structure, per assertion, exactly as depth is. No degree is forced, and none is here claimed for the
physical assertion.

Experimental correspondence: Appendix D.

32 Shared Constituency: The Overlap Theorems
This section establishes shared Constituency.
Dependencies. Construction 16.1; Definitions 5.1, 26.1, 26.2, 26.3, 29.1 and 31.1;
Axiom 5.2; Propositions 5.9 and 14.1; Theorems 1, 9.4, 23.5, 31.5–31.6, 34.2–34.5, 36.2
with Lemmas 26.1 and 28.2; Corollary 8.3; Remark 15.1; Sections 9, 23. Nothing else
is used.

52

Definition 32.1 (Sharing and Its Count) Contents a, b share constituency when their
constitutions have common content — the disjointness of Theorem 18.3 fails. The sharing
count is δ(a, b) := the count of common intercessions, finite and decidable (Proposition 5.9,
Corollary 8.3).
Theorem 32.1 (The Additivity Defect Measures Sharing) For any summable a, b:
r(a ◦ b) = r(a) + r(b) + 1 − δ(a, b)
with Theorem 18.3 the case δ = 0.

Proof The composite’s intercessions are those of a together with those of b and the one
copula (Axiom 5.2, Definition 5.1, as in Theorem 18.3); the count of a union of two finite
collections is the sum less the count of the common members — proven by pairing off the
common members once (bounded induction, Theorem 10.2, over the finite collections).
□
Lemma 32.2 (Edge Accounting) Adding a single adjacency between already-connected contents increases independent circulation by exactly one; an adjacency joining two disconnected
constitutions increases it by none.

Proof Connected case: the new adjacency together with an existing passage between its
endpoints (Theorem 26.2) is a circuit holding a private adjacency — the new one — hence
independent of all prior circulation (the argument of Theorem 29.4); and any circuit using the
new adjacency composes with this one (Definition 29.1, Lemma 29.1) to circulation avoiding
it, so exactly one is added. Disconnected case: the joining adjacency lies on no circuit —
a circuit traversing it would have to return between the two sides without it, and no such
passage exists.
□
Theorem 32.3 (No Circulation Without Overlap) Sharing-free composition creates no circulation: the independent circulation of a composite with disjoint constituents is exactly the
sum of the constituents’ circulations, the junction adjacencies lying on no circuit. Consequently, in any composed plurality, every circuit not interior to a single descent traverses
shared constitution: overlap is the sole source of circulation beyond the descent.

Proof The junction content and its two adjacencies join previously disconnected constitutions: by Lemma 32.2 each contributes nothing, and the total is additive — computationally
verified (38 = 38 on paired depth-six descents), with the junction adjacencies confirmed as
bridges. A circuit crossing between constituents must traverse junction structure or identified content; junction adjacencies lie on no circuit, so cross-circulation exists only where
constitutions are identified — shared.
□
Theorem 32.4 (The Overlap Formula) When a and b share a connected constitution s, the
composite’s circulation is exactly
circ(a ◦ b) = circ(a) + circ(b) − circ(s) + 1

53

Proof The union of the two constitutions, intersecting exactly in s, has adjacency and content
counts given by inclusion–exclusion, whence its circulation is circ(a) + circ(b) − circ(s)
by the same pairing argument as Theorem 32.1 applied to both counts; the shared part
makes the union connected, so the junction’s two adjacencies now join already-connected
contents: the first is a bridge into the isolated junction content, the second closes exactly one
new circuit (Lemma 32.2) — the composite loop running through one branch, the shared
constitution, and the other branch. Total: the stated formula. computationally verified exactly
on identification-built structures at three depth-and-overlap settings (39, 45, 42 — each
matching prediction).
□
Lemma 32.5 (Monotonicity) Sharing never lengthens distances and never shrinks balls:
every passage of the unshared structure remains a passage, so d can only decrease and |B(a,
r)| only grow. computationally verified across all pairs on a shared structure.
Remark 32.1 (The Parallel, Marked as Structural Observation) Within a descent, conjugation
is the sole source of odd circulation (Theorem 27.5); across descents, sharing is the sole source
of any circulation (Theorem 32.3). The two theorems exhibit one principle at two scales:
circulation is co-relation — a content standing in more than one relation at once. The pairing
of a unit with its reversal and the participation of a constitution in two composites are the
two faces of multiplicity of standing, and everything that bends or circulates in the chain’s
geometry has been traced to one of them.

Experimental correspondence: Appendix D.

33 The Plurality’s Circulation: Localization, the
Junction Class, and the Overlap Scale
This section establishes the Plurality’s Circulation.
Dependencies. Construction 16.1; Definitions 26.1, 26.2, 26.3, 28.1, 29.1, 31.1 and 32.1;
Axiom 5.2; Propositions 5.9 and 14.1; Theorems 1, 9.4, 27.4, 31.5–31.6, 32.5, 33.2–33.4,
34.2–34.5, 35.2–35.3, 36.3, 37.2–37.5 with Lemmas 26.1, 28.2, 32.2 and 32.5; Sections
9, 23. Nothing else is used.
Theorem 33.1 (Triangle Generation Extends to Pluralities) In a sharing-free plurality,
and equally in structures whose descents share constitution by identification, all circulation
remains generated by triangles.

Proof Sharing-free: the circulation is the union of the descents’ circulations (Theorem 32.3),
each triangle-generated (Theorem 29.4), and the junction adjacencies lie on no circuit —
nothing new to generate. Identification-shared: the overlap’s circulation is covered by the
graft triangles — at the shared boundary, the shared lateral acquires the completions of
both branches (each branch’s containers enfold both members of the shared pair), and these
enlarged completion families generate the cross-branch circulation exactly; computationally
verified in full: the triangle span equals the circulation at both tested overlap settings (38 =
38 twice), as in the disjoint case.
□

54

Theorem 33.2 (The Junction Class) Composition over a connected shared constitution creates exactly one circulation class outside the triangle span: the composite loop through the
junction. The junction content lies in no triangle (its two departures are non-adjacent whenever the sharing is proper), so every circuit through it is non-triangular; and the gap between
circulation and triangle span is exactly one — computationally verified (39 against 38, at
both settings).

Proof By the overlap formula (Theorem 32.4) the junction adds exactly one independent
circuit; by Theorem 33.1 everything else is triangle-generated; the junction’s absence from all
triangles follows from Theorem 27.1’s dichotomy — its bearing pair is open — so the added
circuit cannot be composed of triangles, which never traverse its adjacencies. The plurality’s
circulation therefore decomposes exactly: the triangle-generated interior, plus one junction
class per composition over connected overlap.
□
Theorem 33.3 (The Overlap Scale) The junction class has a characteristic scale: the
minimal circuit through the junction has circumference exactly
2(N − j) + 2
where N is the descents’ depth and j the shared depth — twice the unshared height, plus the
two junction steps. Deep sharing yields tight circulation; shallow sharing, wide. In every consistent rendering the scale renders as 2(N − j) + 2 bindings of the one datum (Definition 25.1,
Theorem 17.1).

Proof Any circuit through the junction descends one branch from its terminal stratum to
shared content and returns through the other; by the exact spine distances (Theorem 30.1)
each branch traversal to the nearest shared content — the shared top at height j + 1 —
has length at least N − j, attained along the spine; with the two junction adjacencies, the
minimum is 2(N − j) + 2, attained. computationally verified exactly at both settings (12 and
10).
□
Corollary 33.4 (The Forcing Question: No Degree Forced, Every Degree Priced) No growth
degree is privileged by the chain: every degree is attainable (Theorem 31.2) and none is
required. What the chain does force is the price: for a plurality of total content count C, any
degree-d growth window satisfies r^d ≤ κ_d · |B(r)| ≤ κ_d · C — extent is bounded by the
d-th root of capacity. At fixed capacity, ascending degree contracts extent; dimension is not
selected by the framework but budgeted by it, per assertion, exactly as the split of Rule 15.2
requires.

Experimental correspondence: Appendix D.

34 Continuous Curvature as Idealization: The
Constant Density Theorem
This section establishes continuous Curvature as Idealization. (Classical background
for the standard instruments used here: [7]; the derivations below are self-contained
and independent of these sources.)
Dependencies. Axioms 2.3 and 5.2; Definitions 5.1, 25.1, 26.1, 26.2, 26.3, 27.1 and
28.1; Theorems 1, 11.1, 22.2, 22.3, 26.3–26.5, 27.4, 30.3, 31.7, 32.2–32.5, 33.2–33.5,
34.5, 38.1–38.3 with Lemmas 27.4 and 28.2; Sections 9, 23. Nothing else is used.
Definition 34.1 (Defect Content of a Region) A region is any collection of contents of a constitution with its induced adjacencies. Its defect content is the count of minimal circuits lying
within it; its size, the count of its contents. Both are finite and decidable (Proposition 5.9,
Corollary 8.3).
Theorem 34.1 (The Constant Density Theorem) In a closed descent, every interior content
— every stratum and conjugate from level 0 through N − 2 — lies in exactly six minimal
circuits; the terms lie in three, the penultimate pair in four, the terminal stratum in one. The
defect density of the interior is constant.

Proof By enumeration from the census structure (Theorem 28.3, Lemma 28.2). An interior content p at level n lies in: the four triangles on its own lateral, all of which contain
both members; the container-completion of the lateral below, S_n−1, ¬S_n−1, p; and the
constituent-completion of the lateral above, S_n+1, ¬S_n+1, p — six, with the count at
level 0 identical (the term lateral supplying the lower completion). A term lies in its lateral’s
two triangles and completes the level-0 lateral: three. The penultimate contents lie in their
own lateral’s three triangles (the terminal deficit, Theorem 22.3) and complete the lateral
below: four. The terminal stratum lies only as the container-completion of the terminal lateral: one. computationally verified in full at depths six, nine, and twelve — the profile 3, 6,
. . . , 6, 4, 1 without exception.
□
Theorem 34.2 (Depth-Invariance: Refinement Does Not Dilute) The density profile is independent of N and of interior position: deeper assertion neither concentrates nor dilutes defect.
In every consistent rendering, the curvature-per-content of the interior is six quanta of the
one datum, at every depth, in every descent — the descent renders as an object of constant
curvature density, and its idealization at any depth inherits the same nonzero constant.

Proof The counts of Theorem 34.1 make no reference to N except at the four boundary contents; the interior value is six identically, computationally confirmed across depths. Rendering:
each minimal circuit carries exactly one quantum (Theorem 28.1, uniform by Theorem 17.1);
six circuits per interior content is six quanta per content, a count fact preserved by every
consistent rendering (Definition 25.1). No refinement — no increase of N — alters it: the
constant survives every deepening, hence every idealization built on deepening.
□

56

Theorem 34.3 (Curvature Ratios Are Count Ratios) In every consistent rendering, the
comparison of any region’s defect content to any region’s size is a ratio of counts: the datum
scales both and cancels (Theorem 17.2), leaving the comparison in the validated ratio arithmetic (Section 26). Consequently, continuous curvature — curvature as a magnitude varying
over a continuum — exists nowhere primitively and can only ever be the rendering’s idealization of defect-count ratios: the exact theorem-shape of the magnitude gate (Theorem 25.2),
now at the curvature level. What is real in the chain is the counts and their ratios; what is
continuous is the projection’s manner of speaking.

Proof Defect content and size are counts (Definition 34.1); rendered, each is its count of
quanta or of bindings of the single datum (Theorems 28.1 and 17.1); their comparison is
count : count with the datum cancelling — a ratio of Section 26, subject to its cross criterion
and equivalences. Any continuum-valued curvature assigned by a rendering must restrict to
these ratios on count-expressible regions (Definition 25.1) and is therefore their idealization,
exactly as the rendered value of the datum idealizes its convergents (Theorem 25.3).
□

Experimental correspondence: Appendix D.

35 The Conjugation Involution and the Parity
Invariant of Closure
This section establishes the Conjugation Involution and the Parity Invariant of Closure. (Classical background for the standard instruments used here: [8]; the derivations
below are self-contained and independent of these sources.)
Dependencies. Axioms 2.2, 2.3, 5.2 and 5.3; Definitions 5.1, 5.2, 11.1 and 22.1; Propositions 3.1, 5.5, 5.9 and 14.1; Theorems 1, 9.4, 11.1, 27.2–27.4; Corollary 5.11; Sections
9, 23. Nothing else is used.
Definition 35.1 (Constitutional Conjugation) Constitutional conjugation is the map ¬ carrying each content of a descent to its structural opposite: terms to their opposites (Axiom 2.2),
and each stratum or interior conjugate to the oppositely oriented content of equal height
(Axiom 2.3 componentwise, Theorem 12.1). It is defined on every content whose opposite
stands within the constitution.
Theorem 35.1 (Conjugation Is a Fixed-Point-Free Involution, with One Absence) On the
contents of a closed descent of depth N, constitutional conjugation is an involution; it has no
fixed point; and exactly one content — the terminal stratum S_N — has its conjugate absent
from the constitution.

Proof Involution: ¬¬ is identity, since opposition is self-inverse (Axiom 2.3) and reversal is
involutive (Definition 11.1), and the two coincide on strata (Theorem 12.1). Fixed-pointfreeness: a fixed content would satisfy c = ¬c, a content coinciding with its structural
opposite — the coincidence pattern inadmissible in isolation (Axiom 2.1, Proposition 2.1
via Axiom 3.1); no content of an admissible constitution is self-opposite. Explicitly, at every
height the two contents present — a stratum and its interior conjugate — are opposite and

57

distinct (Theorem 1(a)), never equal. The absence: S_N’s conjugate ¬S_N is forced-asserted
(Proposition 3.1) but never enfolded, since its enfolding would require the stratum S_N+1,
which termination forbids (Theorem 22.3, Theorem 5.10). Every other content’s conjugate
is enfolded: for n ≤ N − 1 both S_n and ¬S_n are constituents of S_n+1 (Axiom 5.2,
Axiom 2.3); the terms are mutually opposite and both present. Thus exactly one absence. □
Theorem 35.2 (The Parity Invariant of Closure) The content count of a closed descent of
depth N is odd:
|contents| = 2·P_N + 1
where P_N is the count of conjugate pairs.

Proof By Theorem 35.1 conjugation partitions the contents into two-element orbits c, ¬c
together with the single content whose conjugate is absent, S_N. The orbit count is P_N
and the remainder is one: |contents| = 2·P_N + 1, odd (Section 9 parity of counts). That the
odd content is the terminal stratum is Theorem 35.1’s absence; that its singleness coincides
with lysis’s single unpaired unit is the agreement of two independently derived facts — the
involution’s orbit remainder and the ledger’s frontier — both equal to one, both the terminal
stratum. computationally verified: at depths five, seven, nine, and twelve the content counts
are 13, 17, 21, 27, each odd, each 2·P_N + 1 with P_N the pair count 6, 8, 10, 13.
□
Theorem 35.3 (The Operator Commutes with Conjugation) The emerged second-order
structure (Theorem 24.4) is invariant under constitutional conjugation: conjugation carries
adjacencies to adjacencies and preserves the forced structure, so it is a symmetry of the operator. The operator’s structure therefore respects the conjugate-orbit partition of Theorem 35.1
— its invariant content splits into conjugation-even and conjugation-odd parts.

Proof Conjugation preserves adjacency: enfolding maps to enfolding (¬ carries a containerconstituent pair to the opposite such pair, Axiom 2.3, Axiom 5.2) and co-constituency to coconstituency (the two sectors of a boundary map to the two sectors of the opposite boundary,
Theorem 12.1); it preserves orientation-reversal (Definition 11.1) and radius (Theorem 16.2).
Every component of the second-order constitution (Definition 24.1) is thus carried to a like
component, so conjugation is an automorphism of the forced structure (Theorem 24.4) — a
symmetry of the operator. A structure commuting with an involution decomposes into the
involution’s even and odd parts (the content fixed by the orbit-swap and the content negated
by it), each respected by the structure. computationally verified: the combinatorial realization
of the operator commutes with the conjugation permutation exactly, the terminal stratum
mapping to itself as the involution’s lone absence requires.
□

Experimental correspondence: Appendix D.

36 The Circle and π: Exact Shell Laws, the
Composition Window, and the Unforced Constant
This section establishes the Circle and π. (Classical background for the standard
instruments used here: [7]; the derivations below are self-contained and independent
of these sources.)
Dependencies. Definitions 25.1, 26.1, 26.2, 26.3, 29.1, 30.1 and 31.1; Theorems 22.2,
22.3, 26.3–26.5, 30.3, 31.6, 31.7, 34.2, 35.2–35.3, 36.2 with Corollary 30.3; Sections 9,
23. Nothing else is used.
Definition 36.1 (Closed Circuit, Shell, Circumference) A closed circuit is a passage returning to its origin (Definition 26.3, Lemma 29.1) — the chain’s primitive of the circle: a path
that comes back. The shell Sh(a, r) about a content a is the collection of contents at distance
exactly r (Theorem 26.3); its count is the circumference of radius r about a. The ball is the
shells’ accumulation (Definition 30.1). All quantities are counts.
Theorem 36.1 (No Circle in a Single Descent) In a single descent, the shell about an interior
content has count equal to the valence at radius one and exactly four at every radius from
two through the range window: the circumference does not grow. The line has no circles.

Proof |Sh(a, r)| = |B(a, r)| − |B(a, r − 1)| = (4r + 2) − (4(r−1) + 2) = 4 for r ≥ 2 within the
window of Theorem 30.2, and |Sh(a, 1)| is the valence five (Theorem 26.4). computationally
verified: 5, 4, 4, 4, 4, 4, 4 at depth sixteen. A circle-analog requires growing circumference;
the one-dimensional descent (Corollary 30.3) has none.
□
Theorem 36.2 (The Exact Shell Law of the Sequential Plurality) In the sequential composite
of descents of depth P (Definition 31.1), the circumference about an interior junction is
exactly
|Sh(r)| = 4r − 2 for 2 ≤ r ≤ P + 2
within the junction chain’s reach, with |Sh(1)| = 3 (the junction’s valence: two chain
neighbors and its pendant top).

Proof Every content lies at an exactly computable distance: a junction at chain-distance s
(one at s = 0, two at each s ≥ 1); the pendant top under a junction at s at distance s + 1; and
the pendant pair at depth t ≥ 1 below a top at distance s + 1 + t, two contents per depth
(Theorem 30.1’s exact spine distances, the pendant entered at its terminal stratum). For 2 ≤
r ≤ P + 2, the shell therefore collects: the two junctions at s = r; the center’s own pendant
pair at depth r − 1 (two contents); for each s from 1 through r − 2, two junctions each
contributing a pair (four contents per s); and the two tops at s = r − 1 (one content each).
Total: 2 + 2 + 4(r − 2) + 2 = 4r − 2. computationally verified exactly at every radius from
two through thirteen — 6, 10, 14, 18, 22, 26, 30, 34, 38, 42, 46, 50 — on a forty-four-descent
composite of depth twenty-two.
□

59

Theorem 36.3 (The Composition Window: Planarity Is Scale-Bounded) Beyond the
pendant depth the circumference stops growing: for r ≥ P + 3 (within the chain’s reach),
|Sh(r)| = 4P + 8
exactly — a constant. The sequential plurality is planar only within the window its composition sets; at scales beyond its pendants it is a line. The geometry class of a plurality is
not absolute but windowed by what its assertions compose.

Proof For r ≥ P + 3 the center’s pendant and all near pendants are exhausted (their greatest
depth is P + 1, the terms); only junctions at chain-distance s with r − s − 1 ≤ P + 1
contribute. The shell collects: the two junctions at s = r; the pairs from the two junctions at
each s from r − P − 2 through r − 2 (P + 1 values of s, four contents each); and the two
tops at s = r − 1. Total: 2 + 4(P + 1) + 2 = 4P + 8, independent of r. computationally
verified: with P = 4, shells follow 4r − 2 through r = 6 (22), then hold at exactly 24 = 4·4
+ 8 from r = 7 through r = 19.
□
Theorem 36.4 (π Is Not Forced) No universal circumference-to-radius constant exists at
the primitive level. Within the planar window the ratio is (4r − 2) : r — varying with r, its
rendered limit the lattice constant four of this composition; beyond the window it falls; and
a different composition yields a different law. The chain forces the existence of the circle in
planar pluralities and its exact shell laws; it does not force the proportionality constant, and
any identification of a chain ratio with π would be an unforced selection. None is made.

Proof Within the window, (4r − 2)/r = 4 − 2/r: no single ratio, and its idealization is four
— a constant of the sequential composition, not of the chain, since Theorem 36.3 shows the
same structure leaving planarity entirely beyond its window, and a composition of different
pendant depth P′ has a different window and plateau (4P′ + 8). computationally verified on
two compositions: pendant depth twenty-two gives ratios rising 3.00, 3.50, 3.75, 3.83 toward
four; pendant depth four gives ratios rising to 3.5 then falling through 3.0, 2.0, 1.5 as the
window closes. The ratio is composition- and scale-dependent; by Corollary 31.3 the growth
class itself is assertional, a fortiori its constants. A universal π is therefore not a theorem of
the primitive chain — exactly as the incommensurability discipline (Theorem 25.2) requires:
a continuous constant cannot be read off finite counts, and is not.
□
Remark 36.1 (Where π Legitimately Enters — Stated, Not Performed) The downstream
Light-Constant derivation obtains π as the spectral closure constant of a bounded continuous domain. That derivation is a rendering: it presupposes the continuum idealization
(Definition 25.1) and the emerged operator’s rendered spectrum, both downstream by the
architecture. The value π belongs to a specified rendering — the isotropic continuum idealization of a planar plurality — and its derivation is the projection program’s, not performed
here and not claimed.

Experimental correspondence: Appendix D.

37 Primitive Trigonometry: The Radial
Decomposition, the Additive Identity, and the
Two Circles
This section establishes primitive Trigonometry.
Dependencies. Definitions 5.2, 21.1, 21.2, 25.1, 26.1, 26.2, 26.3, 30.1, 31.1 and
36.1; Theorems 17.1, 17.2, 21.1, 21.2, 21.3, 26.3, 30.1, 31.1, 36.2, 36.3 and 36.4;
Corollary 31.3; Sections 9, 23. Nothing else is used.
Definition 37.1 (The Radial Decomposition) In the sequential plurality, every content of
a shell about an interior junction has two structural components of its distance: the chain
component s — the distance from the center to the content’s attachment junction along
the junction chain — and the pendant component p — the distance from that junction
into the pendant to the content (entry step plus depth; zero for junctions themselves). Both
are counts determined by constitution (Definition 26.1, Theorem 30.1), not coordinates: no
label is assigned (Theorem 16.1); the two components are the two composition directions the
assertion itself contains (Definition 31.1).
Theorem 37.1 (Exactness of the Decomposition, and the Additive Identity) For every shell
content, r = s + p exactly. Consequently, for every interior shell content — both components
positive — the component ratios satisfy, in the validated ratio arithmetic,
(s : r) ⊞ (p : r) = unity
exactly: the primitive trigonometric identity is additive. The Pythagorean composition —
the squares of the component ratios summing to unity — is not primitive: it fails on the chain’s
metric. The axis contents (one component the whole: pure-chain junctions, pure-pendant
descent of the center’s own pendant) are the extremes, where the decomposition is unity in a
single component and no second ratio stands (zero is capacity exhaustion, Definition 5.2, not
a ratio term).

Proof Exactness: every passage from the center to a pendant content factors through the
attachment junction — the junction is a cut content for its pendant (the pendant attaches
by the single entry adjacency, Definition 31.1) — so the minimal passage is the minimal
chain passage followed by the minimal pendant passage, and distances add: r = s + p,
with the pendant leg exact by the spine distances (Theorem 30.1). computationally verified
on every shell content at radii five, eight, and eleven of a forty-four-descent composite: the
decomposition exact without exception, seventy-eight interior contents checked. The additive
identity is then the sum law of ratios with a common accumulation (Theorem 21.3): s + p
= r gives (s : r) ⊞ (p : r) = (s + p : r) = (r : r) = unity (Theorem 21.1). The Pythagorean
failure is exhibited: at s = p = 4, r = 8, the additive composition gives unity while the
squared composition gives one half — computationally confirmed on all checked interior
contents: the squared identity holds at none of them. What the classical identity composes
quadratically, the chain composes additively; the quadratic form belongs to a rendering, not
to the structure.
□
Theorem 37.2 (The Two Circles) Equidistance and closed circulation — the two classical
faces of "the circle" — are distinct primitives that the chain separates. The shell is not a

61

circuit: as a structure it is disconnected — eleven, seventeen, and twenty-three components at
radii five, eight, and eleven (the pendant pairs as two-content arcs, the tops and junctions as
singletons) — so no passage runs around it. And circuits are not equidistant: every triangle
spans two distances from any center (computationally exhibited), and the junction loop spans
many. The classical circle — one object at once equidistant and circulating — exists only
where a rendering identifies the two, and that identification is an isotropic idealization, not
a primitive fact.

Proof Shell disconnectedness: adjacent contents differ in distance by at most one
(Theorem 26.3’s metric) and shell membership fixes the distance, so shell-internal adjacencies are exactly the equal-distance adjacencies — the pendant laterals (Lemma 27.4: laterals
preserve height, and within one pendant, equal depth is equal distance); contents of distinct
pendants are never adjacent (the pendants meet only through junctions, which lie at other
distances); hence the shell decomposes into the lateral pairs and isolated tops and junctions, computationally counted as stated. Circuits non-equidistant: a triangle’s three contents
include an enfolding step, which changes height and hence, from a center aligned with the
chain, distance (computation exhibit: distances 44, 45, 45).
□
Remark 37.1 (Rendered Trigonometry — Located, Not Performed) The continuous sine and
cosine are the isotropic rendering’s idealization of the component ratios of Definition 37.1: a
rendering that identifies the two circles (Theorem 37.2) and composes components isotropically replaces the additive identity with the quadratic one, and the ratios (s : r), (p : r) with
their continuum interpolants. That construction presupposes the continuum idealization (Definition 25.1) and, for the periodic structure, the rendered spectrum of the emerged operator
— the setting of the downstream Light-Constant derivation. Nothing further is claimed.

Experimental correspondence: Appendix D.

38 Functions and Limits: Constitutional
Correspondence and the Primitive Limit
This section establishes functions and Limits.
Dependencies. Definitions 5.2, 21.1, 21.2 and 25.1; Propositions 5.9 and 14.1; Theorems
9.4, 16.1, 23.6, 25.2, 26.3–26.5, 30.3, 30.4, 40.2 with Rule 15.2 and Corollaries 7.3,
16.5, 16.6; Sections 9, 23. Nothing else is used.
Definition 38.1 (Constitutional Correspondence) A correspondence between families of
contents is constitutional when each pairing is determined by constitution alone — the image
standing to the argument in a relation the chain has proven, with no assignment made.
The chain already possesses its constitutional correspondences: conjugation (Definition 35.1,
an involution by Theorem 35.1), the successor and predecessor on counts (Theorem 10.2,
Lemma 18.4), and the address (Theorem 15.3). Each is total on its proven domain, decidable
(Corollary 8.3), and immutable (Proposition 14.1): nothing about it can be chosen, revised,
or lost.

62

Theorem 38.1 (The Residence–Force Split for Functions) Every finite table of pairings
resides in the tower (Theorem 15.1): functions-as-configurations are universally available.
But residing is not being forced: a residing table is an assertional object — instantiated only
where asserted (Rule 15.2) — while the constitutional correspondences of Definition 38.1 are
universal, holding in every descent as theorems. Functions therefore split exactly as everything in the chain has split (Corollary 15.4): a forced universal core — the correspondences
constitution determines — and an assertional remainder — the tables particular assertions
carry. The chain forces no arbitrary function and forbids none; it locates them.

Proof Availability is Theorem 15.1 applied to the table’s configuration. Universality of the
core is the citation of each correspondence’s theorem, none of which mentions any particular
assertion. That the remainder is assertional is Rule 15.2: instantiation follows assertion. The
split is exhaustive by definition — a correspondence is either constitution-determined or not
— and the two classes are nonempty (the core by 43.1; the remainder by any residing table
not constitution-determined, which exists since residence outruns forcing at every depth with
room, Theorem 15.1).
□
Theorem 38.2 (The Exact Gap Law, and the Primitive Limit) The convergent brackets of
Theorem 25.3 obey an exact width law: the gap between consecutive convergents is
(c_k : c_k+1) − (c_k+1 : c_k+2) = ± 1 : (c_k+1 · c_k+2)
exactly — one count over the product — with orientation alternating and width strictly
decreasing. A limit-location is hereby defined: a comparison bracketed from both orientations
by ratios whose defect ratio strictly tightens. The securitizer’s datum is the chain’s first limitlocation (Theorems 25.2 and 25.3): bracketed ever more tightly, from alternating sides, with
arrival impossible — the primitive limit is tightening without arrival, finite at every stage
within capacity (Section 9), and the "limit point" is the rendering’s idealization of the location
(Definition 25.1), exactly as with the datum’s value, curvature, and π.

Proof The gap’s numerator is c_k·c_k+2 − c_k+12 , and by the recursion c_k+2 = c_k+1
+ c_k this equals c_k2 + c_k·c_k+1 − c_k+12 — the unit defect of Theorem 25.3, ±1
with alternating sign; the denominator is the product by the cross criterion (Definition 21.2,
Lemma 20.1). Widths 1 : (c_k+1·c_k+2) strictly decrease since the products strictly increase
(counts strictly increasing, Theorem 10.2). Alternation is the defect’s sign alternation. Arrival
impossible is Theorem 25.2. computationally verified: the gap law exact at every order one
through nineteen, widths strictly decreasing, signs alternating throughout.
□
Theorem 38.3 (Separation: the Bracket Family Distinguishes All Ratios) Any two distinct
ratios are separated by at least one count over the product of their accumulations — (p : q)
and (u : v) distinct differ by at least 1 : (q·v), by the cross criterion — and the bracket widths
fall below any such separation at a finite order within capacity. Hence the bracket family
distinguishes every pair of distinct ratios: no two distinct ratios both survive all brackets, and
the location is unique to arbitrary ratio-fineness — uniqueness of the limit, in the only form
the primitive level admits.

63

Proof Distinctness gives p·v ̸= u·q (Definition 21.2), whole counts differing by at least one,
whence the separation bound after division by the common accumulation q·v (Lemma 20.1).
The widths 1 : (c_k+1·c_k+2) fall below 1 : (q·v) as soon as c_k+1·c_k+2 exceeds q·v,
which occurs at a finite order since the products strictly increase without bound within
capacity (Theorem 10.2, Corollary 5.11’s chain). computationally verified on six pairwisedistinct ratios: separation bounds hold and a finite distinguishing order exists for every pair.
□
Remark 38.1 (Rendered Limits — Located, Not Performed) The continuous limit — a point
attained, a function continuous at it, the calculus built on both — is the rendering’s idealization of the limit-location: the rendering assigns the location a value (as it assigned the
datum its root, Theorem 25.1), and continuity and the derivative are its composition rules
for such values. Block fourteen — the calculus — is thereby located entirely in the rendering,
its primitive substrate now complete.
